 
 [2605.30960] Revisiting Zeroth-Order Hessian Approximation: A Single-Step Policy Optimization Lens 
 Revisiting Zeroth-Order Hessian Approximation: A Single-Step Policy Optimization Lens 
 Junbin Qiu
 Affiliation:  Hong Kong University of Science and Technology (Guangzhou)
 Zhaowei Hong
 Affiliation:  Hong Kong University of Science and Technology (Guangzhou)
 Renzhe Xu
 Affiliation:  Shanghai University of Finance and Economics
 Yao Shu
 Affiliation:  Hong Kong University of Science and Technology (Guangzhou)
 Correspondence to: yaoshu@hkust-gz.edu.cn 
 Abstract 
 Accurate Zeroth-Order (ZO) Hessian estimation is a cornerstone of derivative-free methods, essential for tasks such as bilevel optimization, Bayesian inference, and uncertainty quantification.
However, obtaining a complete suite of low-variance estimators for the Hessian and its inverse in high-dimensional settings remains a significant challenge.
To address this, we propose a unified framework that reinterprets ZO Hessian approximation through the lens of single-step Policy Optimization (PO).
This perspective establishes a theoretical equivalence between general ZO Hessian estimators and the Hessian of a smoothed PO objective, unifying distinct classical randomized estimators as specific instances of baseline selection.
Building on this foundation, we introduce ZoVH, a comprehensive suite of variance-reduced estimators for the full Hessian matrix, its regularized inverse, and the bias-corrected inverse Hessian-gradient product.
ZoVH leverages two key techniques: (1) a unique optimal baseline derived to provably minimize variance, and (2) a query reuse strategy that incorporates historical function queries to enhance sample efficiency without inflating costs.
Our rigorous theoretical analysis confirms the unbiasedness of the Hessian estimator, validates the variance optimality of our baseline, provides error bounds for the entire ZoVH suite, and establishes convergence guarantees for the resulting curvature-aware ZO algorithm.
Extensive empirical results validate our theoretical findings, demonstrating that ZoVH achieves superior estimation accuracy and convergence performance in real-world applications.
Code is available at https://github.com/Qjbtiger/ZoVH . 
 Keywords:  Machine Learning, ICML
 1 Introduction 
 Zeroth-Order (ZO) Hessian approximation for black-box objectives F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ≜ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) ] F({\bm{\theta}})\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}};\xi)] , which are accessible solely through noisy function evaluations, represents a fundamental challenge in derivative-free optimization  (Coope and Tappenden, 2020 ; Balasubramanian and Ghadimi, 2022 ; Roy et al. , 2022 ; Feng and Wang, 2023 ) .
Estimating accurate curvature information is crucial for various applications, including derivative-free bilevel optimization  (Aghasi and Ghadimi, 2025 ; Yang et al. , 2026 ) , uncertainty quantification  (Kalmikov and Heimbach, 2014 ) , and curvature-aware fine-tuning of Large Language Models (LLMs)  (Zhao et al. , 2025 ) .
While naive finite-difference methods typically necessitate 𝒪 ​ ( d 2 ) \mathcal{O}(d^{2}) function queries  (Fabian, 1971 ) , randomized rank-one estimators can reduce the query complexity to a constant number of evaluations, independent of the dimension d d (e.g., 4 4 queries for 2SPSA  (Spall, 2000 ) and 3 3 for RDSA  (L. A. et al. , 2017 ) ).
We provide a discussion of related work about ZO Hessian approximation in Appx.  A . 
 Despite its practical significance, ZO Hessian approximation suffers from high variance, particularly in noisy, high-dimensional regimes  (Zhu, 2022 ; Feng and Wang, 2023 ) .
While a recent study by (Qiu et al. , 2025 ) bridged ZO gradient estimation with single-step Policy Optimization (PO) methods to establish a rigorous theoretical foundation and variance reduction techniques, such connections remain unexplored for ZO Hessian approximation.
Moreover, prior studies have primarily focused on estimating the Hessian ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}}) itself.
In contrast, the estimation of regularized inverse Hessian ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 (\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1} and its product with the gradient ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 ​ ∇ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) (\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}\nabla F({\bm{\theta}}) are essential for curvature-aware zeroth-order optimization  (Zhu, 2022 ; Zhao et al. , 2025 ) , but have received limited attention. 
 Our first contribution is to demonstrate a unified framework for ZO Hessian approximation from the PO perspective.
Within this framework, ZO Hessian approximation is reinterpreted as the Hessian of a smoothed objective ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) under a parameterized sampling policy (Sec.  3.1 ).
Specifically, for Gaussian sampling, this perspective leads to explicit Hessian identities (Props.  3.2 and 3.3 ) incorporating baseline terms.
By selecting different baselines, these identities recover classical Hessian estimators from (Balasubramanian and Ghadimi, 2022 ) as special cases (Sec.  3.2 ).
Furthermore, this framework extends naturally to general sampling distributions via importance sampling (Sec.  3.3 ). 
 Building on this foundation, our second contribution is ZoVH, a novel Zeroth-Order Variance-reduced Hessian approximation that minimizes estimator variance through optimal baseline selection and query reuse (Sec.  4 ).
ZoVH constructs a Hessian estimator leveraging two techniques: (a) an averaged baseline that provably minimizes variance among all constant baselines (Thm.  4.7 ); and (b) a query reuse strategy that estimates the Hessian from a history buffer of past function queries, increasing the sample size without incurring additional query costs, thereby further reducing variance (Sec.  4.1 ).
To assess the efficacy of ZoVH, we provide a comprehensive theoretical analysis in Sec.  4.2 , covering its unbiasedness (Thm.  4.6 ) and bias-variance trade-off (Thm.  4.8 ).
We then present an empirical error analysis in Sec.  6.1 , demonstrating that ZoVH achieves significantly lower estimation error compared to classical Hessian estimators across various synthetic functions and neural networks. 
 Our third contribution lies in the development of a practical framework that extends ZoVH to an efficient curvature-aware Zeroth-Order Optimization (ZOO) algorithm.
Specifically, we develop a stable ZO inverse Hessian approximation by incorporating ridge regularization into the ZoVH estimator (Sec.  5.1 ), and a bias-corrected inverse Hessian-gradient product that mitigates the bias introduced by reusing function queries for both Hessian and gradient estimation (Sec.  5.2 ).
These approximations form the basis of a curvature-aware ZOO algorithm (Alg.  1 ) that efficiently computes second-order updates using minimal function queries.
Theoretically, we prove the convergence guarantee of this approach (Thm.  5.5 ), and empirically, we validate its effectiveness on three representative applications (Sec.  6.2 ), showing substantial improvements over existing ZO methods. 
 2 Preliminaries 
 Problem Setup. 
 We study ZO approximation of the Hessian ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ∈ ℝ d × d \nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d} for the stochastic objective: 
 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ≜ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) ] , F({\bm{\theta}})\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\!\left[f({\bm{\theta}};\xi)\right], 
 (1) 
 where F F is accessible only through noisy function evaluations of f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) f({\bm{\theta}};\xi) , and ξ \xi represents the inherent stochasticity in the function evaluation.
Our goal is to construct a Hessian estimator 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}}) using a small number of function queries, thereby avoiding the prohibitive 𝒪 ​ ( d 2 ) \mathcal{O}(d^{2}) complexity of full finite-difference schemes  (Fabian, 1971 ) , while maintaining low estimation variance. 
 Classical ZO Hessian Estimators. 
 We adopt a unified perspective in which Hessian information is recovered from finite differences of noisy function evaluations along random probing directions.
Several classical ZO Hessian estimators  (Balasubramanian and Ghadimi, 2022 ; Zhu, 2022 ) can be expressed using a common template.
Let { 𝐮 k } k = 1 K ∼ i.i.d. 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 d ) \{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) be standard Gaussian probing directions and μ > 0 \mu>0 be a smoothing parameter.
The One-Point Stein estimator combines a rank-one term with an identity correction derived from Stein’s identity  (Stein, 1972 ) : 
 𝐇 ^ S 1 ≜ 1 K ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) μ 2 ​ ( 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) . \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}_{\text{S}_{1}}\triangleq\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}\left({\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)\ . 
 (2) 
 Following the same principle, the Two-Point Stein estimator replaces the one-point measurement with a one-sided finite difference: 
 𝐇 ^ S 2 ≜ 1 K ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) − f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) μ 2 ​ ( 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) , \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}_{\text{S}_{2}}\triangleq\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)-f({\bm{\theta}};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}\left({\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)\ , 
 (3) 
 and the Three-Point Stein estimator further adopts a symmetric central difference, which typically improves numerical stability: 
 𝐇 ^ S 3 ≜ 1 2 ​ K ​ ∑ k = 1 K \displaystyle\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}_{\text{S}_{3}}\triangleq\frac{1}{2K}\sum_{k=1}^{K} 
 f ​ ( 𝜽 k + ; ξ ) − 2 ​ f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) + f ​ ( 𝜽 k − ; ξ ) μ 2 \displaystyle\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}_{k}^{+};\xi)-2f({\bm{\theta}};\xi)+f({\bm{\theta}}_{k}^{-};\xi)}{\mu^{2}} 
 (4) 
 × ( 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) . \displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left({\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)\ . 
 where 𝜽 k ± ≜ 𝜽 ± μ ​ 𝐮 k {\bm{\theta}}_{k}^{\pm}\triangleq{\bm{\theta}}\pm\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k} . 
 In contrast to the Stein-type estimators, (Balasubramanian and Ghadimi, 2022 ; Zhao et al. , 2025 ) consider a randomized Central-Difference (CD) estimator that employs a rank-one weighted average without the identity correction: 
 𝐇 ^ CD ​ ( 𝜽 ) ≜ 1 2 ​ K ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 k + ; ξ ) − 2 ​ f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) + f ​ ( 𝜽 k − ; ξ ) μ 2 ​ 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ . \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}_{\text{CD}}({\bm{\theta}})\triangleq\frac{1}{2K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}_{k}^{+};\xi)-2f({\bm{\theta}};\xi)+f({\bm{\theta}}_{k}^{-};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}\,{\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}\ . 
 (5) 
 In Sec.  3 , we reinterpret these estimators through the lens of PO, demonstrating that they emerge as special cases of our generalized Hessian estimation framework. 
 ZO Gradient Estimator. 
 In practical ZOO algorithms, Hessian approximation is typically paired with a ZO gradient estimator to enable second-order updates  (Zhu, 2022 ; Zhao et al. , 2025 ) .
We adopt the Gaussian-smoothed gradient estimator from  (Qiu et al. , 2025 ) , which approximates ∇ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla F({\bm{\theta}}) via finite differences along Gaussian probing directions { 𝐮 k } k = 1 K ∼ i.i.d. 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 d ) \{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) : 
 ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ≜ 1 K − 1 ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) − b μ ​ 𝐮 k , \hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}})\triangleq\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)-b}{\mu}\,{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\ , 
 (6) 
 where the averaged baseline b ≜ 1 K ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) b\triangleq\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi) is used to reduce the estimator variance. 
 Notations. 
 Throughout this paper, we denote 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ⋅ ] ≜ 𝔼 𝐮 ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 0 , 𝐈 d ) ​ [ ⋅ ] \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}[\cdot]\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\mathbf{I}}_{d})}\quantity[\cdot] , and 𝔼 ​ [ ⋅ ] ≜ 𝔼 ξ ​ 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ⋅ ] \mathbb{E}\quantity[\cdot]\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\ \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\quantity[\cdot] as the full expectation over both the stochasticity ξ \xi and the standard Gaussian distribution. Furthermore, for a matrix 𝐀 {\mathbf{A}} , we use ‖ 𝐀 ‖ \norm{{\mathbf{A}}} and ‖ 𝐀 ‖ F \norm{{\mathbf{A}}}_{F} to denote its spectral norm and Frobenius norm. 
 3 Rethinking ZO Hessian Approximation 
 In this section, we reframe Zeroth-Order (ZO) Hessian approximation through the lens of Policy Optimization (PO). By interpreting the smoothed objective as an expectation over a sampling policy, we derive a unified Hessian estimator that generalizes classical finite-difference methods (Sec.  3.1 ). This perspective offers a rigorous derivation of existing estimators (Sec.  3.2 ), extends naturally to importance sampling (Sec.  3.3 ), and highlights a potential pathway for developing improved approximation schemes. 
 3.1 The Single-Step Policy Optimization Perspective 
 Following  (Qiu et al. , 2025 ) , we adopt the smoothed objective function F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) defined over a smoothing policy π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}}) . We reformulate this objective within a single-step policy optimization framework by treating π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}}) as a parameterized policy (where 𝐱 = 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 {\mathbf{x}}={\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}} ): 
 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 𝔼 𝐱 ∼ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝐱 ; ξ ) ] ] . F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}\sim\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})}\quantity[\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\mathbf{x}};\xi)]]\ . 
 (7) 
 The Hessian of the PO objective F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) is derived by differentiating the policy gradient: 
 Lemma 3.1 (Hessian of PO objective ( 7 )) . 
 The Hessian of the smoothed objective F μ F_{\mu} in ( 7 ) is given by: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 𝔼 𝐱 ∼ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ [ 𝐇 π 𝜽 ⋅ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝐱 ; ξ ) ] ] , \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}\sim\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})}\left[{\mathbf{H}}_{\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\mathbf{x}};\xi)]\right]\ , 
 where 𝐇 π 𝛉 ≜ ( ∇ ln ⁡ π 𝛉 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ) ​ ( ∇ ln ⁡ π 𝛉 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ) ⊤ + ∇ 2 ln ⁡ π 𝛉 ​ ( 𝐱 ) {\mathbf{H}}_{\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}}\triangleq\left(\nabla\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})\right)\left(\nabla\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})\right)^{\top}+\nabla^{2}\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}}) denotes the Hessian of the policy π 𝛉 \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}} . 
 Remark. The proof is provided in Appx.  C.2 . Lem.  3.1 yields a Hessian estimator for the PO objective ( 7 ) applicable to any differentiable smoothing policy π 𝜽 \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}} . This generality facilitates the derivation of Hessian estimators for diverse smoothing distributions, provided their explicit forms are known. 
 We now consider the canonical case of Gaussian policy, which yields a general Gaussian smoothed Hessian estimators: 
 Proposition 3.2 (Gaussian Smoothed Hessian Estimators) . 
 Let the smoothing policy be Gaussian, i.e., π 𝛉 ​ ( 𝐱 ) = 𝒩 ​ ( 𝛉 , μ 2 ​ 𝐈 d ) \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})={\mathcal{N}}({\bm{\theta}},\mu^{2}\rm{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) . The Hessian of the smoothed objective F μ F_{\mu} in ( 7 ) reduces to: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] − b μ 2 ​ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ] , \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)]-b}{\mu^{2}}\quantity({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d})\right]\ , 
 where the constant baseline b b is an arbitrary term independent of the standard Gaussian random direction 𝐮 ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 d ) {\mathbf{u}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) . 
 Remark. The proof is provided in Appx.  C.3 . Prop.  3.2 is derived by substituting the Gaussian policy π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) = 𝒩 ​ ( 𝜽 , μ 2 ​ 𝐈 d ) \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})={\mathcal{N}}({\bm{\theta}},\mu^{2}{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) into Lem.  3.1 . This derivation does not require explicit invocation of Stein’s identity, as the identity matrix 𝐈 d {\mathbf{I}}_{d} naturally arises from the term ∇ 2 ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) = − 1 μ 2 ​ 𝐈 d \nabla^{2}\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})=-\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{I}}_{d} . Furthermore, since 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ] = 0 \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}[{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}]=0 , the choice of baseline b b is flexible and does not introduce bias. In Sec.  3.2 , we demonstrate that specific choices of b b recover various classical Stein-type Hessian estimators introduced in Sec.  2 . 
 Since 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ] = 0 \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}[{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}]=0 , the identity matrix in Prop.  3.2 can be effectively eliminated by absorbing it into the expectation via baseline subtraction. This observation leads to the following statistically equivalent Hessian estimator: 
 Proposition 3.3 (Rank-One Hessian Estimator) . 
 Let the smoothing policy be Gaussian, i.e., π 𝛉 ​ ( 𝐱 ) = 𝒩 ​ ( 𝛉 , μ 2 ​ 𝐈 d ) \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})={\mathcal{N}}({\bm{\theta}},\mu^{2}\rm{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) . The Hessian of the smoothed objective F μ F_{\mu} in ( 7 ) reduces to: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \displaystyle\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) 
 = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] − b μ 2 ​ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ ] \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)]-b}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\right] 
 with the averaged baseline: 
 b ≜ 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] ] , b\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\left[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)\right]\right]\ , 
 (8) 
 where 𝐮 ≜ ( 𝐱 − 𝛉 ) / μ {\mathbf{u}}\triangleq({\mathbf{x}}-{\bm{\theta}})/\mu is a standard Gaussian random direction. 
 Remark. The proof is provided in Appx.  C.4 .
Unlike the baseline in the gradient estimator ( 6 ), which is often manually introduced for gradient variance reduction, the averaged baseline in Prop.  3.3 emerges naturally from the Hessian derivation.
Specifically, the identity term 𝐈 d {\mathbf{I}}_{d} is absorbed into the expectation using the identity 𝔼 ​ [ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ ] = 𝐈 d \mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}]={\mathbf{I}}_{d} , yielding an estimator structured as an average of rank-one matrices.
In contrast to the flexibility of the baseline choice in Prop.  3.2 , the averaged baseline in Prop.  3.3 is the unique choice that preserves unbiasedness (see Lem.  3.5 ). 
 3.2 Recovery of Classical ZO Hessian Estimators 
 The choice of the baseline b b in Prop.  3.2 plays a pivotal role in determining the structure of the Hessian estimator. By selecting distinct baselines such as the zero baseline ( b = 0 b=0 ) or the anchor baseline ( b = f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) b=f({\bm{\theta}};\xi) ), we can derive the classical Hessian estimators described in Sec.  2 : 
 Corollary 3.4 (Recovery of Stein-Type Estimators) . 
 The general estimator in Prop.  3.2 recovers the following classical unbiased ZO Hessian estimators as special cases: 
 (I) 
 One-Point Stein Estimator: Setting zero baseline b = 0 b=0 yields the estimator defined in ( 2 ). 
 (II) 
 Two-Point Stein Estimator: Setting anchor baseline b = f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) b=f({\bm{\theta}};\xi) yields the estimator defined in ( 3 ). 
 (III) 
 Three-Point Stein Estimator: Applying antithetic sampling { 𝐮 , − 𝐮 } \{{\mathbf{u}},-{\mathbf{u}}\} with the anchor baseline b = f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) b=f({\bm{\theta}};\xi) yields the estimator defined in ( 4 ). 
 Remark. Since Prop.  3.2 holds for any baseline b b independent of 𝐮 {\mathbf{u}} , all Stein-type Hessian estimators recovered above are unbiased estimators of ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) . While the averaged baseline ( 8 ) can also be applied within the framework of Prop.  3.2 , the resulting estimator is mathematically equivalent to the formulation in Def.  4.1 (derived from Prop.  3.3 ). We therefore omit a separate derivation here for brevity (see Appx.  D.1 for further discussion). 
 In contrast to the flexibility of Prop.  3.2 , the estimator in Prop.  3.3 imposes stricter constraints on the baseline to maintain validity. Specifically, to preserve unbiasedness, the averaged baseline ( 8 ) is the unique admissible choice: 
 Lemma 3.5 (Uniqueness of Averaged Baseline) . 
 For the Hessian estimator in Prop.  3.3 , the averaged baseline in ( 8 ) is the unique choice that preserves unbiasedness, i.e., 𝔼 ​ [ 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝛉 ) ] = ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝛉 ) \mathbb{E}[\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})]=\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) . 
 Remark. The proof is provided in Appx.  C.5 . This result highlights the critical role of the averaged baseline in ensuring the unbiasedness of the estimator in Prop.  3.3 . Furthermore, in Thm.  4.7 , we show that the averaged baseline is also optimal for variance minimization. 
 It is natural to inquire whether antithetic sampling can be applied to Prop.  3.3 to construct an unbiased central-difference estimator using the averaged baseline. We find that this is not feasible. Consider one finite sample of the averaged baseline using an antithetic pair { 𝐮 , − 𝐮 } \{{\mathbf{u}},-{\mathbf{u}}\} , given by b = 1 2 ​ ( f ​ ( 𝜽 + ; ξ ) + f ​ ( 𝜽 − ; ξ ) ) b=\frac{1}{2}(f({\bm{\theta}}^{+};\xi)+f({\bm{\theta}}^{-};\xi)) . Substituting this into the estimator yields a zero coefficient, f ​ ( 𝜽 + ; ξ ) + f ​ ( 𝜽 − ; ξ ) − 2 ​ b = 0 f({\bm{\theta}}^{+};\xi)+f({\bm{\theta}}^{-};\xi)-2b=0 , causing the estimator to degenerate. 
 However, if we relax the requirement for unbiasedness with respect to ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) and instead employ the anchor baseline b = f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) b=f({\bm{\theta}};\xi) with antithetic sampling, we recover the classical central-difference estimator: 
 Corollary 3.6 (Recovery of Randomized Central-Difference Estimator ( 5 )) . 
 By applying antithetic sampling { 𝐮 , − 𝐮 } \{{\mathbf{u}},-{\mathbf{u}}\} with the anchor baseline b = f ​ ( 𝛉 ; ξ ) b=f({\bm{\theta}};\xi) , Prop.  3.3 reduces to the classical central difference Hessian estimator ( 5 ). 
 3.3 Generalized Form Through Importance Sampling 
 While Gaussian smoothing offers analytical tractability, optimal performance in ZOO often relies on the geometry of the smoothing distribution, such as uniform distributions on the sphere for gradient bounding. To extend our framework to arbitrary sampling distributions, we leverage Importance Sampling (IS). This approach facilitates the estimation of the Hessian for a target smoothed objective F μ F_{\mu} defined by a target distribution π 𝜽 \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}} , while drawing samples from a distinct proposal distribution ρ \rho . 
 Proposition 3.7 (General Hessian Estimator via Importance Sampling) . 
 Let π 𝛉 = 𝒩 ​ ( 𝛉 , μ 2 ​ 𝐈 d ) \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}={\mathcal{N}}({\bm{\theta}},\mu^{2}\rm{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) be the target smoothing distribution, ρ \rho be a fixed sampling distribution, and b b be the averaged baseline in ( 8 ). Assume supp ​ ( π 𝛉 ) ⊆ supp ​ ( ρ ) \text{supp}(\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}})\subseteq\text{supp}(\rho) . The Hessian of the smoothed objective F μ ​ ( 𝛉 ) F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) in ( 7 ) can be evaluated by sampling 𝐮 ∼ ρ {\mathbf{u}}\sim\rho via: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 𝔼 𝐮 ∼ ρ ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] − b μ 2 ​ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ ⋅ 𝒲 π / ρ ​ ( 𝐮 ) ] , \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}\sim\rho}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)]-b}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{\pi/\rho}({\mathbf{u}})\right]\ , 
 with the averaged baseline via IS: 
 b ≜ 𝔼 𝐮 ∼ ρ ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] ⋅ 𝒲 π / ρ ​ ( 𝐮 ) ] , b\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}\sim\rho}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\left[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)\right]\cdot\mathcal{W}_{\pi/\rho}({\mathbf{u}})\right]\ , 
 where the IS ratio 𝒲 π / ρ ​ ( 𝐮 ) ≜ π 𝛉 ​ ( 𝛉 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ρ ​ ( 𝐮 ) \mathcal{W}_{\pi/\rho}({\mathbf{u}})\triangleq\frac{\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})}{\rho({\mathbf{u}})} . 
 Remark. The proof is in Appx.  C.6 . This result generalizes Prop.  3.3 to arbitrary proposal distributions ρ \rho . By incorporating the IS ratio 𝒲 π / ρ ​ ( 𝐮 ) \mathcal{W}_{\pi/\rho}({\mathbf{u}}) , we can correct for the discrepancy between the target smoothing distribution π 𝜽 \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}} and the sampling distribution ρ \rho . This flexibility allows us to select proposal distributions that align better with the problem geometry, potentially improving estimator efficiency and reducing variance. 
 4 Variance-Reduced Hessian Approximation 
 Building on the foundation in Sec.  3 , we introduce ZoVH, a practical framework for variance-reduced ZO Hessian approximation. This method integrates the Averaged Baseline ( 8 ) with a Query Reuse mechanism to enhance estimation efficiency (Sec.  4.1 ). We then provide a theoretical analysis of its bias and variance properties in Sec.  4.2 . 
 4.1 Hessian Approximation 
 While Prop.  3.3 characterizes the Hessian as an expectation over Gaussian perturbations, a practical implementation necessitates a finite-sample Monte Carlo approximation. Moreover, we incorporate the averaged baseline and query reuse techniques to reduce variance, as detailed below. 
 Averaged Baseline. 
 As established in Lem.  3.5 and Thm.  4.6 , the averaged baseline defined in ( 8 ) is the unique choice that ensures the Hessian estimator remains unbiased. Consequently, we employ the following estimator, constructed from a finite batch of queries using the averaged baseline: 
 Definition 4.1 (Hessian Approximation) . 
 We propose a Hessian approximation 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}}) to approximate ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) in Prop.  3.3 by aggregating a set of K K queries { f ​ ( 𝐱 k ; ξ ) } k = 1 K \{f({\mathbf{x}}_{k};\xi)\}_{k=1}^{K} with Gaussian perturbations 𝐱 k = 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k {\mathbf{x}}_{k}={\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k} and 𝐮 k ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 d ) {\mathbf{u}}_{k}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},\rm{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) via importance sampling: 
 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) ≜ 1 K − 1 ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝐱 k ; ξ ) − b ^ μ 2 ​ 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ ⋅ 𝒲 ​ ( 𝐱 k ) , \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})\triangleq\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\mathbf{x}}_{k};\xi)-\hat{b}}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}\cdot\mathcal{W}({\mathbf{x}}_{k}), 
 (9) 
 where the baseline is b ^ ≜ 1 K ​ ∑ k = 1 K 𝒲 ​ ( 𝐱 k ) ​ f ​ ( 𝐱 k ; ξ ) \hat{b}\triangleq\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{W}({\mathbf{x}}_{k})f({\mathbf{x}}_{k};\xi) , and the importance sampling ratio is 𝒲 ​ ( 𝐱 k ) ≜ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 k ) ρ ​ ( 𝐮 k ) \mathcal{W}({\mathbf{x}}_{k})\triangleq\frac{\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}}_{k})}{\rho({\mathbf{u}}_{k})} . 
 Remark. The normalization factor 1 K − 1 \frac{1}{K-1} applies a bias correction to account for the use of the sample mean b ^ \hat{b} (see Thm.  4.6 ). Additionally, self-normalized importance sampling is utilized to enhance numerical stability and robustness. In Thm.  4.7 , we further demonstrate that the averaged baseline is optimal for variance minimization under common sampling distributions. 
 Query Reuse. 
 Adopting the strategy from  (Qiu et al. , 2025 ) , the query reuse technique significantly reduces estimator variance by aggregating historical queries from recent optimization steps. Specifically, we maintain a history buffer ℋ t \mathcal{H}_{t} that stores the most recent N × K N\times K queries, comprising the random directions { 𝐮 t − i , k } i , k = 1 N , K \{{\mathbf{u}}_{t-i,k}\}_{i,k=1}^{N,K} and their corresponding function values { y t − i , k ≜ f ​ ( 𝜽 t − i + μ ​ 𝐮 t − i , k ; ξ t − i ) } i , k = 1 N , K \{y_{t-i,k}\triangleq f({\bm{\theta}}_{t-i}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{t-i,k};\xi_{t-i})\}_{i,k=1}^{N,K} . Notably, the memory overhead introduced by this mechanism is negligible in practice, as the history buffer requires storing only scalar function values and random seeds, rather than high-dimensional random vectors (see Appx.  E.1 ). 
 By integrating the averaged baseline with query reuse technique, we define the complete ZoVH estimator as follows: 
 Definition 4.2 (ZoVH (Zeroth-Order Variance-Reduced Hessian Estimator)) . 
 At iteration t t , given the history buffer ℋ t \mathcal{H}_{t} containing the most recent N × K N\times K queries, the Hessian approximation is defined as: 
 𝐇 ^ ≜ 1 N ​ K − 1 ​ ∑ i , k = 1 N , K y t − i , k − b ^ t μ 2 ​ 𝐮 t − i , k ​ 𝐮 t − i , k ⊤ , \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}\triangleq\frac{1}{NK-1}\sum_{i,k=1}^{N,K}\frac{y_{t-i,k}-\hat{b}_{t}}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{u}}_{t-i,k}{\mathbf{u}}_{t-i,k}^{\top}\ , 
 (10) 
 where the averaged baseline is b ^ t ≜ 1 N ​ K ​ ∑ i , k = 1 N , K y t − i , k \hat{b}_{t}\triangleq\frac{1}{NK}\sum_{i,k=1}^{N,K}y_{t-i,k} . 
 Remark. From the perspective of policy optimization in reinforcement learning, reusing past queries can be interpreted as a form of off-policy learning, where historical data is leveraged to refine current estimates. This perspective aligns with the importance sampling framework established in Prop.  3.7 , wherein the proposal distribution ρ \rho corresponds to the off-policy distribution induced by historical perturbations, distinct from the target distribution π 𝜽 \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}} (which remains a Gaussian distribution centered at the current parameter 𝜽 {\bm{\theta}} ). Consequently, Def.  4.2 can be viewed as a special case of Def.  4.1 , where the importance sampling ratio 𝒲 ​ ( 𝐱 ) \mathcal{W}({\mathbf{x}}) is implicitly integrated into the historical queries. 
 4.2 Theoretical Analysis 
 To facilitate the theoretical analysis, we focus on the simplified setting of N = 1 N=1 (i.e., without query reuse) in Def.  4.2 . This choice is motivated by the observation that in local optimization, parameters typically exhibit slow evolution over short time horizons. Consequently, the proposal distribution ρ \rho closely approximates the target distribution π 𝜽 \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}} , implying that the importance sampling ratio 𝒲 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ≈ 1 \mathcal{W}({\mathbf{x}})\approx 1 and that historical queries are approximately drawn from the target (on-policy) distribution. We also provide a theoretical analysis for the general case of N > 1 N>1 in Appx.  B , which follows analogous reasoning. 
 We first adopt the following standard assumptions regarding the objective function f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) f({\bm{\theta}};\xi) : 
 Assumption 4.3 (Lipschitz Continuity) . 
 ∀ 𝜽 , 𝜽 ′ ∈ ℝ d \forall{\bm{\theta}},{\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , 
 | f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) − f ​ ( 𝜽 ′ ; ξ ) | ≤ \displaystyle|f({\bm{\theta}};\xi)-f({\bm{\theta}}^{\prime};\xi)|\leq 
 L 0 ​ ‖ 𝜽 − 𝜽 ′ ‖ \displaystyle L_{0}\norm{{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}} 
 (11) 
 ‖ ∇ f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) − ∇ f ​ ( 𝜽 ′ ; ξ ) ‖ ≤ \displaystyle\norm{\nabla f({\bm{\theta}};\xi)-\nabla f({\bm{\theta}}^{\prime};\xi)}\leq 
 L 1 ​ ‖ 𝜽 − 𝜽 ′ ‖ \displaystyle L_{1}\norm{{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}} 
 ‖ ∇ 2 f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) − ∇ 2 f ​ ( 𝜽 ′ ; ξ ) ‖ F ≤ \displaystyle\norm{\nabla^{2}f({\bm{\theta}};\xi)-\nabla^{2}f({\bm{\theta}}^{\prime};\xi)}_{F}\leq 
 L 2 ​ ‖ 𝜽 − 𝜽 ′ ‖ . \displaystyle L_{2}\norm{{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}}\ . 
 Assumption 4.4 (Bounded Variance) . 
 ∀ 𝜽 ∈ ℝ d \forall{\bm{\theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

 (12) 
 We then restate the gradient and Hessian of the Gaussian smoothed objective ( 7 ) to support our subsequent analysis: 
 Lemma 4.5 (Smoothed Gradient and Hessian) . 
 For a smoothing parameter μ > 0 \mu>0 , the gradient and Hessian of the smoothed objective F μ ​ ( 𝛉 ) F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) with Gaussian smoothing distribution 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 d ) {\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) satisfy: 
 ∇ F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \displaystyle\nabla F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) 
 = 1 μ ​ 𝔼 𝐮 ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 d ) ​ [ F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ​ 𝐮 ] \displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\mathbf{I}}_{d})}\quantity[F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}){\mathbf{u}}]

 Based on Lem.  4.5 , we prove the unbiasedness of the Hessian estimator ( 9 ): 
 Theorem 4.6 (Bias of Hessian Estimator) . 
 The Hessian estimator 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝛉 ) \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}}) in ( 9 ) is an unbiased estimator of the Hessian of the smoothed objective ( 4.5 ): 
 𝔼 ​ [ 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) ] = ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) . \mathbb{E}[\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})]=\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})\ . 
 Remark. The proof is provided in Appx.  C.8 . Thm.  4.6 extends Lem.  3.5 by demonstrating the unbiasedness of the proposed Hessian estimator ( 9 ) with respect to ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) when employing the averaged baseline. Notably, the bias relative to the true Hessian ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}}) persists, stemming from the inherent discrepancy between ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) and ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}}) (see Thm.  4.8 and Appx.  D.2 ). 
 Theorem 4.7 (Optimal Baseline) . 
 The optimal baseline b ∗ b^{*} that minimizes the variance of the Hessian estimator Var ​ ( 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝛉 ) ) ≜ 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝛉 ) − ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝛉 ) ‖ F 2 ] \mathrm{Var}\quantity(\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}}))\triangleq\mathbb{E}\quantity[\norm{\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})-\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})}_{F}^{2}] is: 
 b ∗ = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ​ ‖ ( 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ‖ F 2 ] 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ‖ ( 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ‖ F 2 ] . b^{*}=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}}\quantity[F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})\norm{\quantity({\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d})}_{F}^{2}]}{\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}}\quantity[\norm{\quantity({\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d})}_{F}^{2}]}\ . 
 (13) 
 In particular, when 𝐮 ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 0 , 𝐈 d ) {\mathbf{u}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) as d → ∞ d\to\infty , such that ‖ ( 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ‖ F 2 \norm{\quantity({\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d})}_{F}^{2} is effectively constant, the optimal baseline reduces to: 
 b ∗ = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ] = F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) . b^{*}=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}}\quantity[F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})]=F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})\ . 
 Remark. The proof is provided in Appx.  C.9 . Thm.  4.7 provides theoretical justification for using the averaged baseline in our Hessian estimator. It presents the explicit form of the optimal baseline that minimizes estimator variance without making assumptions about the distribution of random directions. If the random directions are sampled from standard Gaussian distribution, ‖ ( 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ‖ F 2 \norm{\quantity({\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d})}_{F}^{2} concentrates around the constant d ​ ( d + 1 ) d(d+1) for large dimensions d d . Thus, the averaged baseline remains a robust approximation of the optimal baseline. Besides, the optimal baseline is also related to control-variate view (detailed in Appx.  D.3 ). 
 Theorem 4.8 (Bias-Variance Decomposition of Hessian Estimator) . 
 Under Assump.  4.3 and 4.4 , with the optimal baseline b = F μ ​ ( 𝛉 ) b=F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) (as in Thm.  4.7 ) and 𝐮 ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 d ) {\mathbf{u}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) , the expected Frobenius norm error of the Hessian estimator 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝛉 ) \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}}) with respect to the true Hessian ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝛉 ) \nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}}) is bounded by: 
 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) − ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ F 2 ] ≤ K ​ d ​ ( d + 2 ) ​ V ( K − 1 ) 2 ​ μ 4 ⏟ Variance + L 2 2 ​ μ 2 ​ d ⏟ Squared Bias , \mathbb{E}\quantity[\norm{\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})-\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})}_{F}^{2}]\leq\underbrace{\frac{Kd(d+2)V}{(K-1)^{2}\mu^{4}}}_{\text{Variance}}+\underbrace{L_{2}^{2}\mu^{2}d}_{\text{Squared Bias}}\ , 
 where V ≜ σ ξ 2 + 4 ​ L 0 2 ​ μ 2 ​ ( d + 2 ) V\triangleq\sigma_{\xi}^{2}+4L_{0}^{2}\mu^{2}(d+2) . 
 Remark. The proof is provided in Appx.  C.10 . Thm.  4.8 characterizes the bias-variance trade-off of the Hessian estimator. The variance term captures stochastic fluctuations σ ξ 2 \sigma_{\xi}^{2} and the Gaussian smoothing effect (the second term in V V ), while the squared bias term quantifies the discrepancy between the smoothed and true Hessians. Notably, the variance decreases with a larger smoothing radius μ \mu and increased random directions K K , whereas the bias increases with μ \mu . Consequently, selecting an appropriate smoothing radius μ \mu is critical for minimizing the overall error. 
 5 Efficient Inverse Hessian Approximations 
 Although the Hessian estimator in Def.  4.1 is valuable, practical curvature-aware ZO algorithms typically require the inverse Hessian or its product with the gradient. In this section, we extend ZoVH to derive efficient approximations for both the inverse Hessian (Sec.  5.1 ) and the inverse Hessian-gradient product (Sec.  5.2 ) by leveraging the structure of our estimator. 
 To simplify notation, we omit the explicit dependence on the iteration t t throughout this section. Furthermore, when query reuse is employed, K K denotes the total number of queries, encompassing both current and historical samples. 
 5.1 Inverse Hessian Approximation 
 By utilizing the Woodbury matrix identity  (Woodbury, 1950 ) , we can efficiently compute the inverse of the Hessian estimator in Def.  4.1 without explicitly inverting a dense matrix: 
 Definition 5.1 (Inverse Hessian Approximation) . 
 Let 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}}) be the Hessian estimator and b ^ \hat{b} be the averaged baseline ( 8 ). Define the scalar ν k ≜ 𝒲 ​ ( 𝐱 k ) ​ ( f ​ ( 𝐱 k ; ξ ) − b ^ μ 2 ) \nu_{k}\triangleq\mathcal{W}({\mathbf{x}}_{k})\left(\frac{f({\mathbf{x}}_{k};\xi)-\hat{b}}{\mu^{2}}\right) , the matrix 𝐔 ≜ [ 𝐮 1 , … , 𝐮 K ] {\mathbf{U}}\triangleq[{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{K}] , and 𝐃 ≜ diag ​ ( ν 1 K − 1 , … , ν K K − 1 ) {\mathbf{D}}\triangleq\mathrm{diag}\left(\frac{\nu_{1}}{K-1},\dots,\frac{\nu_{K}}{K-1}\right) . Then for any regularization parameter λ > 0 \lambda>0 , the exact inverse of the regularized Hessian estimator is: 
 ( 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 = 1 λ ​ 𝐈 d − 1 λ 2 ​ 𝐔 ​ ( 𝐃 − 1 + 1 λ ​ 𝐔 ⊤ ​ 𝐔 ) − 1 ​ 𝐔 ⊤ . \left(\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)^{-1}=\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{I}}_{d}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\,{\mathbf{U}}\Big({\mathbf{D}}^{-1}+\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}{\mathbf{U}}\Big)^{-1}{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}\ . 
 (14) 
 Further approximating 𝐔 ⊤ ​ 𝐔 {\mathbf{U}}^{\top}{\mathbf{U}} by diag ​ ( 𝐔 ⊤ ​ 𝐔 ) \mathrm{diag}({\mathbf{U}}^{\top}{\mathbf{U}}) yields the approximation of ( 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 \left(\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)^{-1} as below: 
 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ≜ 1 λ ​ 𝐈 d − ∑ k = 1 K ν k λ 2 ​ ( K − 1 ) + λ ​ ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ​ 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ . \widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})\triangleq\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{I}}_{d}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\nu_{k}}{\lambda^{2}(K-1)+\lambda\,\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2}}\,{\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}\ . 
 (15) 
 Note that this approximation becomes exact if the random directions { 𝐮 k } k = 1 K \{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K} are pairwise orthogonal. 
 Remark. The detailed derivation is provided in Appx.  C.11 . Since 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}}) is rank- K K (where K ≪ d K\ll d ), the regularization term λ ​ 𝐈 d \lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d} is essential for invertibility. Strict equality in ( 15 ) holds when the random directions are pairwise orthogonal. This condition is naturally satisfied by uniform spherical or coordinate-basis sampling and is approximately satisfied by standard Gaussian sampling when the dimension d d is large.
In Appx.  D.4 , we further quantify the gap between the exact inverse ( 14 ) and the approximation ( 15 ) under standard Gaussian sampling, showing that the theoretical approximation error is 𝒪 p ​ ( K / d ) \mathcal{O}_{p}(K/\sqrt{d}) and empirically decays as d − 1 / 2 d^{-1/2} for fixed small K K . 
 We then analyze the error of the inverse Hessian approximation 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) \widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}}) defined in ( 15 ) with respect to the inverse of the true Hessian ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}}) : 
 Theorem 5.2 (Bias of Inverse Hessian Estimator) . 
 Assume that there exists a positive constant ρ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \rho\in(0,1] such that | ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 / λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + 1 | ≥ ρ |\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2}/\lambda(K-1)+1|\geq\rho for all k ∈ [ K ] k\in[K] . Under Assump.  4.3 and the orthogonality of random directions { 𝐮 k } k = 1 K \{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K} , the bias of the inverse Hessian approximation in ( 15 ) with respect to the inverse of the true Hessian is bounded by: 
 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) − ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 ‖ F 2 ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\quantity[\norm{\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})-\quantity(\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}}^{2}_{F}] 
 ≤ \displaystyle\leq 
 d λ 2 ​ ρ 2 ​ ( σ min + λ ) 2 ​ ( K ​ d ​ ( d + 2 ) ​ V ( K − 1 ) 2 ​ μ 4 + L 2 2 ​ μ 2 ​ d ) , \displaystyle\frac{d}{\lambda^{2}\rho^{2}\quantity(\sigma_{\min}+\lambda)^{2}}\quantity(\frac{Kd(d+2)V}{(K-1)^{2}\mu^{4}}+L_{2}^{2}\mu^{2}d)\ , 
 where σ min \sigma_{\min} denotes the minimum eigenvalue of the true Hessian ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝛉 ) \nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}}) . 
 Remark. The proof is provided in Appx.  C.13 . Thm.  5.2 bounds the approximation error, showing it depends on the Hessian estimation error (Thm.  4.8 ) scaled by three factors: (1) The regularization parameter λ \lambda , ensuring the invertibility and stability. A larger λ \lambda reduces the bound but may over-smooth curvature, necessitating a balanced choice. (2) The minimum eigenvalue σ min \sigma_{\min} of the true Hessian. A larger σ min \sigma_{\min} leads to a smaller error bound. (3) The constant ρ \rho , ensuring non-singularity in the Woodbury update. In high dimensions where 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ 𝐮 ‖ 2 ] ≈ d \mathbb{E}[\norm{{\mathbf{u}}}^{2}]\approx d , the term | ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 / λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + 1 | |\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2}/\lambda(K-1)+1| is typically sufficient to guarantee a reasonable ρ \rho . 
 5.2 Inverse Hessian-Gradient Product Approximation 
 Combining the ZO gradient estimator ( 6 ) with the inverse Hessian approximation ( 15 ), we compute the product 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ​ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}}) . We consider two sampling strategies based on whether the gradient and Hessian estimators share the same set of random directions: 
 Regime 1: Decoupled Sampling. 
 When the Hessian and gradient estimators use independent sets of random directions, they are statistically uncorrelated, allowing direct multiplication of gradient estimator ( 6 ) and Hessian estimator ( 15 ). However, this doubles the query complexity to 2 ​ K 2K evaluations per iteration, which may be impractical. 
 Regime 2: Shared Sampling. 
 Using the same directions { 𝐮 k } k = 1 K \{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K} for both estimators reduces the cost to K K evaluations but introduces correlation bias. To mitigate this while maintaining efficiency, we propose a bias-corrected estimator: 
 Definition 5.3 (Bias-Corrected Shared Product) . 
 Assume 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) \widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}}) and ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}}) share the same standard Gaussian directions { 𝐮 k } k = 1 K \{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K} . The bias-corrected product approximation is defined as: 
 𝐇 ~ − 1 ( 𝜽 ) ∇ ^ F ( 𝜽 ) ≜ ∑ k = 1 K μ ν k ( 1 λ ​ ( K − 1 ) \displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}})\triangleq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mu\nu_{k}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\lambda(K-1)} 
 (16) 
 − 𝐮 k ⊤ ​ ( ∑ k ′ = 1 , k ′ ≠ k K ν k ′ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ) / ( K − 2 ) λ 2 ​ ( K − 1 ) + λ ​ ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ) 𝐮 k . \displaystyle\quad\quad\left.-\frac{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}\left(\sum_{k^{\prime}=1,k^{\prime}\neq k}^{K}\nu_{k^{\prime}}{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}}\right)/(K-2)}{\lambda^{2}(K-1)+\lambda\,\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2}}\right){\mathbf{u}}_{k}. 
 Remark. The derivation is given in Appx.  C.12 . We use a leave-one-out strategy to remove self-interaction terms ν k ​ 𝐮 k \nu_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k} , significantly reducing bias. Defining the global weighted sum 𝐬 ≜ ∑ j = 1 K ν j ​ 𝐮 j {\mathbf{s}}\triangleq\sum_{j=1}^{K}\nu_{j}{\mathbf{u}}_{j} , the interaction term can be efficiently computed as 𝐮 k ⊤ ​ ( ∑ j ≠ k ν j ​ 𝐮 j ) = 𝐮 k ⊤ ​ 𝐬 − ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 {\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}(\sum_{j\neq k}\nu_{j}{\mathbf{u}}_{j})={\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}{\mathbf{s}}-\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2} . This allows computation in 𝒪 ​ ( K ​ d ) \mathcal{O}(Kd) time, avoiding the 𝒪 ​ ( K 2 ​ d ) \mathcal{O}(K^{2}d) cost of pairwise operations. Additionally, since the directions are reused, no extra function evaluations are needed. 
 Advantages. 
 Def.  5.3 provides an efficient framework for curvature-aware zeroth-order optimization with minimal query overhead, as outlined in Alg.  1 . This approach offers several advantages: (a) it preserves efficiency by sharing query directions for both gradient and Hessian estimation, requiring only K K function evaluations per iteration, and is identical to the cost of  (Zhu, 2022 ; Zhao et al. , 2025 ) ; (b) it benefits from variance reduction via an averaged baseline, and can be further enhanced by integrating a query reuse mechanism, as detailed in Appx.  D.5 ; and (c) it is easy to implement, requiring only a straightforward modification to standard ZO optimizers to incorporate the product approximation in Def.  5.3 . 
 Algorithm 1 ZoVH for Curvature-Aware Zeroth-Order Optimization 
 0:  Initial parameter 𝜽 0 {\bm{\theta}}_{0} , smoothing parameter μ \mu , regularization parameter λ \lambda , learning rate η \eta , history size N N , number of random directions K K , total iterations T T .
 1:  Initialize history buffer ℋ ← ∅ \mathcal{H}\leftarrow\emptyset .
 2:   for t = 0 t=0 to T − 1 T-1 do 
 3:   Sample K K random directions { 𝐮 t , k } k = 1 K \{{\mathbf{u}}_{t,k}\}_{k=1}^{K} .
 4:   Evaluate values { y t , k ≜ f ​ ( 𝜽 t + μ ​ 𝐮 t , k ; ξ t ) } k = 1 K \{y_{t,k}\triangleq f({\bm{\theta}}_{t}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{t,k};\xi_{t})\}_{k=1}^{K} .
 5:   Update history buffer: ℋ ← ℋ ∪ { ( 𝐮 t , k , y t , k ) } k = 1 K \mathcal{H}\leftarrow\mathcal{H}\cup\{({\mathbf{u}}_{t,k},y_{t,k})\}_{k=1}^{K} .
 6:   Maintain buffer size: if | ℋ | > N ​ K |\mathcal{H}|>NK , remove the oldest K K entries.

Thm.  5.4 shows that the error bound of the inverse Hessian-gradient product approximation shares the same structure as that of the inverse Hessian approximation in Thm.  5.2 , differing only by constant factors B 1 B_{1} and B 2 B_{2} , since the biased-corrected multiplication in ( 16 ) mitigates the correlation-induced bias.

 Theorem 5.5 (Convergence of Alg.  1 (Informal)) . 
 Under Assump.  4.3 and  4.4 , employing the optimal baseline b t b_{t} from Thm.  4.7 , with η ∼ 𝒪 ​ ( ϵ 2 ) \eta\sim\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2}) and T ∼ 𝒪 ​ ( ϵ − 4 ) T\sim\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{-4}) , we have:

 Remark. The formal statement is provided in Thm.  C.9 and detailed proof are available in Appx.  C.15 . Thm.  5.5 establishes the convergence of ZoVH to a stationary point, up to a bias term induced by the Gaussian smoothing. By appropriately tuning the learning rate η \eta , iteration count T T , and smoothing parameter μ \mu , the algorithm can achieve convergence to an ϵ \epsilon -stationary point. 
 6 Experiments 
 We evaluate the performance of ZoVH through empirical Hessian error analysis on synthetic functions and a neural network (Sec.  6.1 ), convergence benchmarks on synthetic optimization (Sec.  6.2 ) and black-box adversarial attack (Sec.  6.3 ). Additional experiments on curvature-aware zeroth-order LLM fine-tuning and ablation study of the averaged baseline and query reuse techniques are detailed in Appx.  E . 
 6.1 Empirical Error Analysis of Hessian Approximation 
 We first compare the empirical Frobenius norm error of various Hessian estimators against the ground-truth Hessian. We assess the two-point ( 3 ) and three-point ( 4 ) Stein Estimators, the randomized central-difference estimator ( 5 ), and ZoVH (both with and without query reuse technique). The one-point Stein estimator ( 2 ) is excluded due to its significantly higher error than other methods. 
 Figure 1 : Comparison of convergence among different ZO Hessian optimization algorithms on four synthetic functions. All curves are averaged over 10 independent runs. 
 Synthetic Functions. 
 We employ three synthetic benchmarks: Quadratic, Rosenbrock, and Styblinski-Tang functions. For each, we initialize 𝜽 0 ∈ ℝ d {\bm{\theta}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} ( d = 5000 d=5000 ) and perform gradient descent steps, collecting 25 25 test points along the trajectory (detailed in Appx.  F.1 ).
Fig.  2 demonstrates that ZoVH with N = 6 N=6 (utilizing query reuse) consistently yields the lowest estimation error. Even ZoVH with N = 1 N=1 (without query reuse), outperforms all baselines, achieving an 8 × 8\times accuracy improvement over the central-difference estimator ( 5 ) on Quadratic and Rosenbrock functions, and 3.4 × 3.4\times on Styblinski-Tang. The reduced error bars for ZoVH with N = 6 N=6 further confirm the variance reduction efficacy of query reuse. 
 Figure 2 : Frobenius norm Hessian error on three synthetic functions. Results are averaged over 20 20 random initializations and 25 25 test points along each optimization trajectory. The outliers are defined as points outside 1.5 times the interquartile range. 
 Neural Network Hessian. 
 We extend our evaluation to a Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) trained on MNIST  (LeCun et al. , 1998 ) . The network comprises two convolutional and two fully connected layers. We collect 1875 1875 test points along a complete SGD training trajectory (detailed in Appx.  F.1 ).
The results in Fig.  3 reveals that ZoVH with N = 4 N=4 (utilizing query reuse) consistently achieves the lowest estimation error across all layers. Notably, for the second fully connected layer ( Fc2.weight ), ZoVH with N = 4 N=4 achieves a 9 × 9\times error reduction compared to the central-difference estimator. 
 Figure 3 : Frobenius norm Hessian error across CNN layers. Results averaged over 3 3 independent runs and 1875 1875 test points per training trajectory. The outliers are defined as points outside 1.5 times the interquartile range. 
 6.2 Synthetic Function Optimization 
 We then evaluate the convergence of ZoVH against Vanilla ZOO  (Nesterov and Spokoiny, 2017 ) , HiZOO  (Zhao et al. , 2025 ) , and ZoAR  (Qiu et al. , 2025 ) on four synthetic functions: Quadratic, Levy, Rosenbrock, and Ackley (detailed in Appx.  F.2 ). As shown in Fig.  1 , ZoVH with N = 4 N=4 (utilizing query reuse) significantly outperforms baselines in both convergence speed and final accuracy. Notably, ZoVH achieves an average speedup of 22 × 22\times over Vanilla ZOO, underscoring its efficiency in high-dimensional ZOO. 
 6.3 Black-Box Adversarial Attack 
 We further evaluate the performance of ZoVH in the domain of black-box adversarial attacks, a prominent application of zeroth-order optimization  (Zhu, 2022 ; Shu et al. , 2023 ) .
In this scenario, the objective is to identify an optimal perturbation δ \delta for a given input image x x such that a target black-box model misclassifies x + δ x+\delta .
The attack is performed using a convolutional neural network (CNN) trained on the MNIST dataset  (LeCun et al. , 1998 ) .
The compared methods are the same as synthetic experiments in Sec.  6.2 (detailed in Appx.  F.3 ).
To evaluate the efficiency of the algorithms, we measure the minimum number of iterations to achieve a successful attack.
The results in Tab.  1 show that ZoVH with N = 4 N=4 (utilizing query reuse) consistently requires fewer iterations compared to other baseline methods, indicating its superior efficiency in generating adversarial examples.
Specifically, ZoVH with N = 1 N=1 (w/o query reuse) achieves a 3 × 3\times speedup over Vanilla ZOO, while ZoVH with N = 4 N=4 (utilizing query reuse) further achieves a 4 × 4\times speedup. 
 Table 1 : Comparison of the minimal number of iterations to achieve a successful attack for different ZO Hessian methods. Results are averaged over 10 runs. The speedup is compared against the Vanilla ZOO.

 7 Conclusion 
 We introduced a unified framework connecting zeroth-order Hessian approximation with single-step policy optimization to derive ZoVH, a variance-reduced Hessian estimator. Leveraging an optimal baseline and query reuse, ZoVH minimizes estimation error efficiently without increasing the query budget. We further developed stable inverse Hessian approximations for a practical curvature-aware zeroth-order algorithm with proven convergence guarantees. Experiments across synthetic benchmarks, adversarial attacks, and LLM fine-tuning confirm that ZoVH achieves superior convergence speed and accuracy, providing a robust foundation for curvature-aware derivative-free optimization. 
 Impact Statement 
 This paper presents a novel perspective on zeroth-order Hessian approximation framed through single-step policy optimization. Our findings contribute to the theoretical foundations of derivative-free optimization and have the potential to enhance the efficiency of algorithms used in black-box optimization settings. We do not anticipate any specific negative ethical implications or adverse societal impacts resulting from this work.

 Appendix A Related Works 
 Derivative-Free Hessian Approximation. 
 Early efforts on derivative-free Hessian approximation date back to coordinate-wise perturbation schemes that form second-order updates by probing each coordinate direction, which typically requires on the order of 𝒪 ​ ( d 2 ) \mathcal{O}(d^{2}) function evaluations per iteration for an d d -dimensional problem  (Fabian, 1971 ) .
To reduce this query cost, subsequent work moved from coordinate perturbations to random perturbations for curvature estimation.
A representative milestone is Spall’s second-order SPSA (2SPSA, Spall ( 2000 ) ), which estimates the Hessian using only four function evaluations per update, independent of the dimension, yielding a substantial improvement over earlier 𝒪 ​ ( d 2 ) \mathcal{O}(d^{2}) query constructions.
In a similar spirit, randomized finite difference methods were developed for estimating Hessian. One notable line of work is Random Directions Stochastic Approximation (RDSA, L. A. et al. ( 2017 ) ), which employs central sampling to construct Hessian estimates with uniform or asymmetrical Bernoulli distributed directions.
Furthermore, Balasubramanian and Ghadimi ( 2022 ) extended the central sampling approach to Gaussian perturbations, deriving a family of unbiased ZO Hessian estimators via Stein’s identity.
On the application side, ZO Hessian approximations have been leveraged to improve query efficiency and optimization performance in black-box adversarial attacks  (Ye et al. , 2025 ) and in curvature-aware ZO fine-tuning of large language models  (Zhao et al. , 2025 ) . 
 Variance-Reduced Zeroth-Order Optimization. 
 Zeroth-Order Optimization (ZOO) aims to minimize black-box objectives using only function evaluations, and has been extensively studied due to its broad applicability when derivatives are unavailable.
A classical line of work constructs ZO gradient estimators via randomized smoothing and finite differences, including Gaussian smoothing  (Nesterov and Spokoiny, 2017 ) , uniform sampling on the unit sphere  (Flaxman et al. , 2005 ) , and coordinate-wise perturbations  (Lian et al. , 2016 ) .
While these estimators are simple and widely used, they typically suffer from high variance in noisy and high-dimensional regimes, leading to slow convergence and substantial query complexity.
To mitigate this issue, recent studies develop variance-reduced ZO methods by leveraging past queries to reduce the variance of ZO gradient estimates  (Shu et al. , 2023 ; Wang et al. , 2024 ; Qiu et al. , 2025 ) .
Another complementary direction proposes new optimization paradigms that are variance-efficient by design, thereby improving ZO optimization convergence rate and final performance  (Shu et al. , 2025 ) .
Our work is most closely related to variance reduction in ZOO, but differs in that we focus on the approximation of second-order information, i.e., the Hessian matrix. 
 Appendix B Theoretical Analysis for the General Case N > 1 N>1 
 We now extend the theoretical analysis to the query-reuse setting with N > 1 N>1 . In this case, the estimator uses a history buffer of N ​ K NK function queries rather than only the K K queries from the current iteration. This larger sample size reduces the variance of the Hessian estimator, while the use of historical queries introduces an additional error because those queries were generated around previous iterates. The analysis below makes this bias-variance trade-off explicit. We first state the resulting Hessian estimation bound (Thm.  B.1 ) and then explain how the same bound affects the inverse Hessian approximation, the inverse Hessian-gradient product, and the convergence guarantee. 
 Theorem B.1 (Bias-Variance Decomposition for N > 1 N>1 ) . 
 Under Assump.  4.3 and  4.4 , with the optimal baseline b = F μ ​ ( 𝛉 ) b=F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) and 𝐮 ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 d ) {\mathbf{u}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) . Let N > 1 N>1 , the expected Frobenius norm error of the query-reuse Hessian estimator 𝐇 ^ t ​ ( 𝛉 t ) \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}_{t}({\bm{\theta}}_{t}) at iteration t t defined in Def.  4.2 is bounded by: 
 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) − ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ F 2 ] ≤ N ​ K ​ d ​ ( d + 2 ) ​ λ 2 ​ V λ 2 ​ ( N ​ K − 1 ) 2 ​ μ 4 − L 0 2 ​ η 2 ​ μ 2 ​ K ​ ( N 2 − 1 ) ​ d ​ ( d + 2 ) / 3 + L 2 2 ​ μ 2 ​ d , \mathbb{E}\quantity[\norm{\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})-\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})}_{F}^{2}]\leq\frac{NKd(d+2)\lambda^{2}V}{\lambda^{2}(NK-1)^{2}\mu^{4}-L_{0}^{2}\eta^{2}\mu^{2}K(N^{2}-1)d(d+2)/3}+L_{2}^{2}\mu^{2}d\ , 
 where V ≜ σ ξ 2 + 4 ​ L 0 2 ​ μ 2 ​ ( d + 2 ) V\triangleq\sigma_{\xi}^{2}+4L_{0}^{2}\mu^{2}(d+2) . 
 Remark. The proof is provided in Appx.  C.16 . Thm.  B.1 extends the bias-variance decomposition in Thm.  4.8 to the N > 1 N>1 (query reuse) setting. Compared with the N = 1 N=1 case, reusing queries across N N iterations increases the effective sample size from K K to N ​ K NK , reducing the leading variance term through the factor ( N ​ K − 1 ) 2 (NK-1)^{2} . This gain is offset by the additional drift term L 0 2 ​ η 2 ​ μ 2 ​ K ​ ( N 2 − 1 ) ​ d ​ ( d + 2 ) / 3 L_{0}^{2}\eta^{2}\mu^{2}K(N^{2}-1)d(d+2)/3 in the denominator, which captures the error induced by reusing historical queries generated at previous iterates. When N = 1 N=1 , this drift term vanishes and the bound recovers Thm.  4.8 . Therefore, for a small learning rate η \eta and a moderate history length N N , query reuse can reduce the Hessian estimation error and improve the constants in the convergence bound of Thm.  5.5 , while an overly large N N or rapid parameter updates may offset the variance reduction. 
 Extension to Inverse Hessian Approximation. 
 The same resolvent-identity argument used in Appx.  C.13 gives the corresponding inverse Hessian bound. Let the N > 1 N>1 Hessian estimation error bound be 
 ℰ H , N ≜ N ​ K ​ d ​ ( d + 2 ) ​ λ 2 ​ V λ 2 ​ ( N ​ K − 1 ) 2 ​ μ 4 − L 0 2 ​ η 2 ​ μ 2 ​ K ​ ( N 2 − 1 ) ​ d ​ ( d + 2 ) / 3 + L 2 2 ​ μ 2 ​ d . \mathcal{E}_{H,N}\triangleq\frac{NKd(d+2)\lambda^{2}V}{\lambda^{2}(NK-1)^{2}\mu^{4}-L_{0}^{2}\eta^{2}\mu^{2}K(N^{2}-1)d(d+2)/3}+L_{2}^{2}\mu^{2}d. 
 (17) 
 With this notation, the inverse Hessian approximation satisfies 
 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ 𝐇 ~ N − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) − ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 ‖ F 2 ] ≤ d λ 2 ​ ρ 2 ​ ( σ min + λ ) 2 ​ ℰ H , N . \mathbb{E}\quantity[\norm{\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}_{N}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})-\quantity(\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}}^{2}_{F}]\leq\frac{d}{\lambda^{2}\rho^{2}\quantity(\sigma_{\min}+\lambda)^{2}}\mathcal{E}_{H,N}\ . 
 (18) 
 Relative to Thm.  5.2 , query reuse mainly replaces the K K -sample Hessian error by the N ​ K NK -sample error in ℰ H , N \mathcal{E}_{H,N} . The regularization factor λ \lambda , the non-singularity constant ρ \rho , and the spectral factor σ min + λ \sigma_{\min}+\lambda play the same stability roles as in the N = 1 N=1 analysis. When the history buffer is moderate and the iterate drift is small, ℰ H , N \mathcal{E}_{H,N} can be smaller than the N = 1 N=1 counterpart. If N N or η \eta is too large, the drift term in the denominator may offset this benefit. 
 Extension to Inverse Hessian-Gradient Product Approximation. 
 The same reasoning applies to the bias-corrected inverse Hessian-gradient product in Def.  D.3 . The leave-one-out correction is computed over the whole history buffer, so each current or historical query ( i , k ) (i,k) excludes all other pairs ( j , k ′ ) ≠ ( i , k ) (j,k^{\prime})\neq(i,k) rather than only the other directions in the current iteration. Query reuse therefore increases the effective sample size in both the Hessian estimate and the shared inverse-Hessian-gradient product. Historical samples are still generated around previous iterates, so rapid parameter changes or an overly large buffer can make the reused samples stale. This is the same off-policy drift captured in Thm.  B.1 . Query reuse can improve the estimator when N N and η \eta are moderate, but the benefit can be offset if the history distribution moves too far from the current target distribution. 
 Implication for Convergence. 
 This extension also clarifies the convergence guarantee in Thm.  5.5 . The convergence bound depends on the estimation error of the inverse Hessian-gradient product, which is in turn controlled by the quality of the Hessian estimator and the variance bound in Thm.  B.1 . Improving the Hessian estimate through the optimal baseline and query reuse therefore improves the constants in the convergence bound. The resulting iteration complexity remains the standard 𝒪 ​ ( ϵ − 4 ) {\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-4}) zeroth-order rate when N N and K K are fixed. The theoretical role of query reuse is to explain the practical speedup through better estimator quality and smaller constants, rather than through a different asymptotic order. 
 Appendix C Proofs 
 C.1 Useful Lemmas 
 Lemma C.1 (Lipschitz Continuity for Objective) . 
 ∀ 𝜽 1 , 𝜽 2 ∈ ℝ d \forall{\bm{\theta}}_{1},{\bm{\theta}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} , the objective ( 1 ) satisfies:

 (19) 
 ‖ ∇ F ​ ( 𝜽 1 ) − ∇ F ​ ( 𝜽 2 ) ‖ ≤ \displaystyle\norm{\nabla F({\bm{\theta}}_{1})-\nabla F({\bm{\theta}}_{2})}\leq 
 L 1 ​ ‖ 𝜽 1 − 𝜽 2 ‖ , \displaystyle L_{1}\norm{{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{2}}\ , 
 ‖ ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 1 ) − ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 2 ) ‖ ≤ \displaystyle\norm{\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}}_{1})-\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}}_{2})}\leq 
 L 2 ​ ‖ 𝜽 1 − 𝜽 2 ‖ . \displaystyle L_{2}\norm{{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{2}}\ . 
 Proof. 
 Since F ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) ] F({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}};\xi)] , the Lipschitz continuity of F ​ ( 𝜽 ) F({\bm{\theta}}) follows: 
 | F ​ ( 𝜽 1 ) − F ​ ( 𝜽 2 ) | = | 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 1 ; ξ ) − f ​ ( 𝜽 2 ; ξ ) ] | ​ ≤ ( a ) ​ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ | f ​ ( 𝜽 1 ; ξ ) − f ​ ( 𝜽 2 ; ξ ) | ] ​ ≤ ( b ) ​ L 0 ​ ‖ 𝜽 1 − 𝜽 2 ‖ , |F({\bm{\theta}}_{1})-F({\bm{\theta}}_{2})|=|\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}_{1};\xi)-f({\bm{\theta}}_{2};\xi)]|\overset{(a)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[|f({\bm{\theta}}_{1};\xi)-f({\bm{\theta}}_{2};\xi)|]\overset{(b)}{\leq}L_{0}\norm{{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{2}}\ , 
 (20) 
 where ( a ) (a) follows from Jensen’s inequality, and ( b ) (b) comes from Lem.  C.1 . 
 The Lipschitz continuity for the gradient and Hessian of F ​ ( 𝜽 ) F({\bm{\theta}}) can be proved similarly.
∎ 
 Lemma C.2 (Bias of Gradient Estimator ( 6 )) . 
 Let smoothed objective F μ ​ ( 𝛉 ) F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) be ( 7 ), and the gradient estimator ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝛉 ) \hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}}) be ( 6 ), we have: 
 𝔼 ​ [ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ] = ∇ F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) , and ‖ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) − ∇ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ 2 ≤ μ 2 ​ L 1 2 ​ d 2 4 . \mathbb{E}[\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}})]=\nabla F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})\ ,\quad\text{and}\quad\norm{\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}})-\nabla F({\bm{\theta}})}^{2}\leq\frac{\mu^{2}L_{1}^{2}d^{2}}{4}\ . 
 (21) 
 Proof. 
 The unbiasedness of the gradient estimator ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}}) follows from Stein’s identity: 
 𝔼 ​ [ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ] = \displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}})]= 
 𝔼 ​ [ 1 K − 1 ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) − 1 K ​ ∑ k ′ = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ; ξ ) μ ​ 𝐮 k ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)-\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}};\xi)}{\mu}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\right] 
 (22) 
 = \displaystyle= 
 𝔼 ​ [ 1 K − 1 ​ ∑ k = 1 K K − 1 K ​ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) − 1 K ​ ∑ k ′ = 1 , k ′ ≠ k K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ; ξ ) μ ​ 𝐮 k ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\frac{K-1}{K}f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)-\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1,k^{\prime}\neq k}^{K}f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}};\xi)}{\mu}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\right] 
 = \displaystyle=

 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ν 2 ​ ‖ 𝐮 ‖ 4 ] ≤ d ​ ( d + 2 ) μ 4 ​ ( σ ξ 2 + 4 ​ L 0 2 ​ μ 2 ​ ( d + 2 ) ) . \mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}}[\nu^{2}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}}^{4}]\leq\frac{d(d+2)}{\mu^{4}}\quantity(\sigma_{\xi}^{2}+4L_{0}^{2}\mu^{2}(d+2))\ . 
 (33) 
 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ν 2 ​ ‖ 𝐮 ‖ 6 ] ≤ d ​ ( d + 2 ) ​ ( d + 4 ) μ 4 ​ ( σ ξ 2 + 4 ​ L 0 2 ​ μ 2 ​ ( d + 3 ) ) . \mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}}[\nu^{2}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}}^{6}]\leq\frac{d(d+2)(d+4)}{\mu^{4}}\quantity(\sigma_{\xi}^{2}+4L_{0}^{2}\mu^{2}(d+3))\ . 
 (34) 
 ∎ 
 Below are some useful lemmas for N > 1 N>1 case, which can be proved similarly as above.

 C.2 Proof of Lem.  3.1 
 Proof. 
 We first derive the first derivative of the single-step policy optimization objective ( 7 ) by applying the Policy Gradient Thm.  (Sutton et al. , 1999 ) : 
 ∇ F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 𝔼 𝐱 ∼ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ [ ∇ ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝐱 ; ξ ) ] ] . \nabla F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}\sim\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})}\left[\nabla\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\mathbf{x}};\xi)]\right]\ . 
 (48) 
 Taking the second derivative, we have: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = \displaystyle\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})= 
 ∇ ​ ∫ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ ∇ ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝐱 ; ξ ) ] ​ 𝑑 𝐱 ​ = ( a ) ​ ∫ ( ∇ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ ( ∇ ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ) ⊤ + π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ ∇ 2 ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ) ​ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝐱 ; ξ ) ] ​ 𝑑 𝐱 \displaystyle\nabla\int\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})\nabla\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\mathbf{x}};\xi)]d{\mathbf{x}}\overset{(a)}{=}\int\quantity(\nabla\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})\quantity(\nabla\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}}))^{\top}+\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})\nabla^{2}\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}}))\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\mathbf{x}};\xi)]d{\mathbf{x}} 
 (49) 
 = ( b ) \displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=} 
 ∫ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ ( ( ∇ ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ) ​ ( ∇ ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ) ⊤ + ∇ 2 ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ) ​ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝐱 ; ξ ) ] ​ 𝑑 𝐱 \displaystyle\int\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})\quantity(\quantity(\nabla\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}}))\quantity(\nabla\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}}))^{\top}+\nabla^{2}\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}}))\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\mathbf{x}};\xi)]d{\mathbf{x}}

 where ( a ) (a) comes from the product rule of differentiation, and ( b ) (b) follows from the fact that ∇ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) = π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ ∇ ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) \nabla\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})=\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})\nabla\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}}) .
∎ 
 C.3 Proof of Prop.  3.2 
 Proof. 
 Taking the reparametrization trick 𝐱 = 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 {\mathbf{x}}={\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}} with 𝐮 ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 0 , 𝐈 d ) {\mathbf{u}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) , we have: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) ​ = ( a ) ​ 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ​ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] μ 2 ] , \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})\overset{(a)}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\left({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)]}{\mu^{2}}\right]\ , 
 (50) 
 where ( a ) (a) comes from ∇ ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) = 1 μ 2 ​ ( 𝐱 − 𝜽 ) = 1 μ ​ 𝐮 \nabla\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}({\mathbf{x}}-{\bm{\theta}})=\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathbf{u}} and ∇ 2 ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) = − 1 μ 2 ​ 𝐈 d \nabla^{2}\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})=-\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{I}}_{d} for Gaussian policy. 
 Since 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ ] = 𝐈 d \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}[{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}]={\mathbf{I}}_{d} , for any constant baseline b b that is independent of 𝐮 {\mathbf{u}} , ( 50 ) can be further transformed as: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ​ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] μ 2 ] + b ⋅ 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ] = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ​ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) − b ] μ 2 ] , \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\left({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)]}{\mu^{2}}\right]+b\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\left({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\left({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)-b]}{\mu^{2}}\right]\ , 
 (51) 
 which completes the proof.
∎ 
 C.4 Proof of Prop.  3.3 
 Proof. 
 Regardless of the choice of baseline b b , since 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ ] = 𝐈 d \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}[{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}]={\mathbf{I}}_{d} , Prop.  3.2 can always be transformed as: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = \displaystyle\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})= 
 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) − b μ 2 ​ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ ] − 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) − b μ 2 ] ​ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)-b}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)-b}{\mu^{2}}\right]{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\right]

 which completes the proof.

 C.5 Proof of Lem.  3.5 
 Proof. 
 Suppose there exists another baseline b ′ ≠ 𝔼 𝐮 ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 0 , 𝐈 d ) ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] ] b^{\prime}\neq\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\mathbf{I}}_{d})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\left[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)\right]\right] that also satisfies the unbiasedness: 
 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) − b ′ ] μ 2 ​ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ ] = ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] μ 2 ​ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ] . \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\left[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)-b^{\prime}\right]}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\right]=\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\left[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)\right]}{\mu^{2}}\quantity({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d})\right]\ . 
 (53) 
 Take the difference on both sides, we have: 
 0 = \displaystyle 0= 
 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) − b ′ ] μ 2 ​ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ ] − 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] μ 2 ​ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\left[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)-b^{\prime}\right]}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\left[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)\right]}{\mu^{2}}\quantity({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d})\right] 
 (54) 
 = \displaystyle= 
 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ − b ′ μ 2 ​ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ ] + 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] μ 2 ​ 𝐈 d ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[-\frac{b^{\prime}}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\left[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)\right]}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right] 
 = \displaystyle= 
 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] ] − b ′ μ 2 ​ 𝐈 d , \displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\left[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)\right]\right]-b^{\prime}}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\ , 
 which implies that b ′ = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] ] b^{\prime}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\left[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)\right]\right] , contradicting the assumption. Therefore, the average baseline is the unique choice that guarantees the unbiasedness of the Hessian estimator in Prop.  3.3 .
∎ 
 C.6 Proof of Prop.  3.7 
 Proof. 
 We rewrite the smoothed objective using the importance weight w ​ ( 𝐱 ) = π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) / ρ ​ ( 𝐱 ) w({\mathbf{x}})=\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})/\rho({\mathbf{x}}) . The Hessian is ∇ 2 ​ ∫ f ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ρ ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ ρ ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ 𝑑 𝐱 \nabla^{2}\int f({\mathbf{x}})\frac{\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})}{\rho({\mathbf{x}})}\rho({\mathbf{x}})d{\mathbf{x}} . Since ρ \rho is independent of 𝜽 {\bm{\theta}} , the derivatives apply solely to π 𝜽 \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}} . Interchanging differentiation and integration yields 𝔼 𝐱 ∼ ρ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ 1 ρ ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ ∇ 2 π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) ] \mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}\sim\rho}[f({\mathbf{x}})\frac{1}{\rho({\mathbf{x}})}\nabla^{2}\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}})] . The result follows by expanding ∇ 2 π 𝜽 = π 𝜽 ​ ( ∇ 2 ln ⁡ π 𝜽 + ∇ ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ​ ∇ ln ⁡ π 𝜽 ⊤ ) \nabla^{2}\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}=\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}(\nabla^{2}\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}+\nabla\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}\nabla\ln\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}^{\top}) . 
 We start from the Hessian of the smoothed objective in ( 50 ): 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ​ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] μ 2 ] = ∫ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ​ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] μ 2 ​ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ​ 𝑑 𝐮 , \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\left({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)]}{\mu^{2}}\right]=\int\left({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)]}{\mu^{2}}\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})d{\mathbf{u}}\ , 
 (55) 
 where we rewrite the expectation over 𝐮 {\mathbf{u}} as an integral over the Gaussian policy π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝐱 ) \pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\mathbf{x}}) . 
 Under the assumptions supp ​ ( π 𝜽 ) ⊆ supp ​ ( ρ ) \text{supp}(\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}})\subseteq\text{supp}(\rho) that ensures the importance sampling ratio 𝒲 π / ρ ​ ( 𝐮 ) ≜ π 𝜽 ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ρ ​ ( 𝐮 ) \mathcal{W}_{\pi/\rho}({\mathbf{u}})\triangleq\frac{\pi_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})}{\rho({\mathbf{u}})} is well-defined, we can express the Hessian of smoothed objective as: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = ∫ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ​ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] μ 2 ​ 𝒲 π / ρ ​ ( 𝐮 ) ​ ρ ​ ( 𝐮 ) ​ 𝑑 𝐮 = 𝔼 𝐮 ∼ ρ ​ [ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ​ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] μ 2 ​ 𝒲 π / ρ ​ ( 𝐮 ) ] . \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\int\left({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)]}{\mu^{2}}\mathcal{W}_{\pi/\rho}({\mathbf{u}})\rho({\mathbf{u}})d{\mathbf{u}}=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}\sim\rho}\left[\left({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)]}{\mu^{2}}\mathcal{W}_{\pi/\rho}({\mathbf{u}})\right]\ . 
 (56) 
 Finally, similar to the derivation in ( 50 ), we have: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 𝔼 𝐮 ∼ ρ ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] − 𝔼 𝐮 ∼ ρ ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] ​ 𝒲 π / ρ ​ ( 𝐮 ) ] μ 2 ​ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ ⋅ 𝒲 π / ρ ​ ( 𝐮 ) ] . \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}\sim\rho}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)]-\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}\sim\rho}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)]\mathcal{W}_{\pi/\rho}({\mathbf{u}})\right]}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{\pi/\rho}({\mathbf{u}})\right]\ . 
 (57) 
 ∎ 
 C.7 Proof of Lem.  4.5 
 Proof. 
 The gradient of the smoothed objective F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) is a direct result of Stein’s identity: 
 ∇ F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ∇ F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ] = 1 μ ​ 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ∇ 𝐮 F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ] ​ = ( a ) ​ 1 μ ​ 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ​ 𝐮 ] , \nabla F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\nabla F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})\right]=\frac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{u}}F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})\right]\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}){\mathbf{u}}\right]\ , 
 (58) 
 where ( a ) (a) follows from Stein’s identity. 
 Then, the Hessian of the smoothed objective F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) can be derived by taking another derivative: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = ( 1 μ ​ 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ( ∇ F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ) ​ 𝐮 ] ) = 1 μ 2 ​ 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ( ∇ 𝐮 F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ) ​ 𝐮 ⊤ ] . \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\quantity(\frac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\quantity(\nabla F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})){\mathbf{u}}\right])=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\quantity(\nabla_{\mathbf{u}}F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})){\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\right]\ . 
 (59) 
 Note that ∇ 𝐮 ( F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ​ 𝐮 ) = ( ∇ 𝐮 F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ) ​ 𝐮 ⊤ + F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ​ 𝐈 d \nabla_{\mathbf{u}}\quantity(F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}){\mathbf{u}})=\quantity(\nabla_{\mathbf{u}}F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})){\mathbf{u}}^{\top}+F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}){\mathbf{I}}_{d} , we have: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 1 μ 2 ​ 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ∇ 𝐮 ( F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ​ 𝐮 ) − F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ​ 𝐈 d ] ​ = ( a ) ​ 1 μ 2 ​ 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ​ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ] , \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{u}}\quantity(F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}){\mathbf{u}})-F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}){\mathbf{I}}_{d}\right]\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})\quantity({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d})\right]\ , 
 (60) 
 where ( a ) (a) again follows from Stein’s identity.
∎

 Finally, following the discussion in Appx.  D.1 , which establishes that Prop.  3.2 with an averaged baseline is equivalent to Def.  4.1 , we conclude that the Hessian estimator in Def.  4.1 also attains the minimum variance among all constant baselines.
∎
