 C.11 Derivation of Def.  5.1 
 Proof. 
 The Hessian estimator in Def.  4.1 can be rewritten as: 
 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 1 K − 1 ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) − 1 K ​ ∑ k ′ = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ; ξ ) μ 2 ​ 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ ⋅ 𝒲 ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ) = U ​ D ​ U ⊤ , \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})=\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)-\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}\cdot\mathcal{W}({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k})=UDU^{\top}\ , 
 (71) 
 where we denote 𝐃 = diag ⁡ ( ν 1 / ( K − 1 ) , … , ν K / ( K − 1 ) ) ∈ ℝ K × K {\mathbf{D}}=\operatorname{diag}(\nu_{1}/\quantity(K-1),\ldots,\nu_{K}/\quantity(K-1))\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K\times K} with ν k = f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) − 1 K ​ ∑ k ′ = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ; ξ ) μ 2 ​ 𝒲 ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ) \nu_{k}=\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)-\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}\mathcal{W}({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k}) , and 𝐔 = [ 𝐮 1 , 𝐮 2 , … , 𝐮 K ] ∈ ℝ d × K {\mathbf{U}}=[{\mathbf{u}}_{1},{\mathbf{u}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}_{K}]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times K} . 
 According to the Woodbury matrix identity (Woodbury, 1950 ) , we have: 
 ( 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 = ( λ ​ 𝐈 d + 𝐔𝐃𝐔 ⊤ ) − 1 = 1 λ ​ 𝐈 d − 1 λ 2 ​ 𝐔 ​ ( 𝐃 − 1 + 1 λ ​ 𝐔 ⊤ ​ 𝐔 ) − 1 ​ 𝐔 ⊤ . \quantity(\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}=\quantity(\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d}+{\mathbf{U}}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{U}}^{\top})^{-1}=\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{I}}_{d}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}{\mathbf{U}}\quantity({\mathbf{D}}^{-1}+\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}{\mathbf{U}})^{-1}{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}\ . 
 (72) 
 If we consider the diagonal approximation of 𝐔 {\mathbf{U}} (i.e. assuming { 𝐮 k } k = 1 K \{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K} are orthogonal), then 𝐔 ⊤ ​ 𝐔 {\mathbf{U}}^{\top}{\mathbf{U}} is a diagonal matrix with the k k -th diagonal element being ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 \norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2} . The inverse term can be calculated as: 
 𝐃 − 1 + 1 λ ​ 𝐔 ⊤ ​ 𝐔 = diag ⁡ ( K − 1 ν k + 1 λ ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ) k = 1 K . {\mathbf{D}}^{-1}+\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}{\mathbf{U}}=\operatorname{diag}\quantity(\frac{K-1}{\nu_{k}}+\frac{1}{\lambda}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2})_{k=1}^{K}\ . 
 (73) 
 Therefore, the approximated inverse Hessian 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) \widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}}) is: 
 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 1 λ ​ 𝐈 d − 1 λ 2 ​ ∑ k = 1 K 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ K − 1 ν k + 1 λ ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 = 1 λ ​ 𝐈 d − ∑ k = 1 K ν k λ ​ ( λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ) ​ 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ . \widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})=\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{I}}_{d}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}}{\frac{K-1}{\nu_{k}}+\frac{1}{\lambda}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2}}=\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{I}}_{d}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\nu_{k}}{\lambda\quantity(\lambda(K-1)+\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2})}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}\ . 
 (74) 
 ∎ 
 C.12 Derivation of Def.  5.3 
 Proof. 
 Adopt from inverse Hessian estimator in ( 15 ), we have: 
 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ​ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) = ( 1 λ ​ 𝐈 d − ∑ k = 1 K μ ​ ν k λ ​ ( λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ) ​ 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ ) ​ ∇ ^ ​ F . \widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}})=\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{I}}_{d}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mu\nu_{k}}{\lambda\quantity(\lambda(K-1)+\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2})}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}\right)\hat{\nabla}F\ . 
 (75) 
 The first term is simple: 
 1 λ ​ 𝐈 d ​ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 1 λ ​ ( K − 1 ) ​ ∑ k = 1 K μ ​ ν k ​ 𝐮 k . \frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{I}}_{d}\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}})=\frac{1}{\lambda(K-1)}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mu\nu_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\ . 
 (76) 
 While for the second term, to reduce the bias introduced by the correlation between the same random directions used in Hessian and gradient estimation, we remove the random direction that is used in the Hessian estimation from the gradient estimation, i.e., we have: 
 ( ∑ k = 1 K μ ​ ν k λ ​ ( λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ) ​ 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ ) ​ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) = ( ∑ k = 1 K μ ​ ν k λ ​ ( λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ) ​ 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ ) ​ ( 1 K − 2 ​ ∑ k ′ = 1 k ′ ≠ k K μ ​ ν k ′ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ) \displaystyle\quantity(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mu\nu_{k}}{\lambda\quantity(\lambda(K-1)+\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2})}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top})\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}})=\quantity(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mu\nu_{k}}{\lambda\quantity(\lambda(K-1)+\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2})}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top})\quantity(\frac{1}{K-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime}=1\\
k^{\prime}\neq k\end{subarray}}^{K}\mu\nu_{k^{\prime}}{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}}) 
 (77) 
 = \displaystyle= 
 1 K − 2 ​ ∑ k = 1 K ∑ k ′ = 1 k ′ ≠ k K μ ​ ν k ​ μ ​ ν k ′ λ ​ ( λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ) ​ 𝐮 k ​ ( 𝐮 k ⊤ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ) = ∑ k = 1 K μ ​ ν k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ ​ ( ∑ k ′ = 1 , k ′ ≠ k K ν k ′ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ) / ( K − 2 ) λ ​ ( λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ) ​ 𝐮 k . \displaystyle\frac{1}{K-2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime}=1\\
k^{\prime}\neq k\end{subarray}}^{K}\frac{\mu\nu_{k}\mu\nu_{k^{\prime}}}{\lambda\quantity(\lambda(K-1)+\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2})}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\quantity({\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mu\nu_{k}\frac{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}\quantity(\sum_{k^{\prime}=1,k^{\prime}\neq k}^{K}\nu_{k^{\prime}}{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}})/\quantity(K-2)}{\lambda\quantity(\lambda(K-1)+\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2})}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\ . 
 Combining the two terms together, we have: 
 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ​ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) = ∑ k = 1 K μ ​ ν k ​ ( 1 λ ​ ( K − 1 ) − 𝐮 k ⊤ ​ ( ∑ k ′ = 1 , k ′ ≠ k K ν k ′ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ) / ( K − 2 ) λ ​ ( λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ) ) ​ 𝐮 k . \widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mu\nu_{k}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda(K-1)}-\frac{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}\quantity(\sum_{k^{\prime}=1,k^{\prime}\neq k}^{K}\nu_{k^{\prime}}{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}})/\quantity(K-2)}{\lambda\quantity(\lambda(K-1)+\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2})}\right){\mathbf{u}}_{k}\ . 
 (78) 
 ∎ 
 C.13 Proof of Thm.  5.2 
 Proof.

 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) − ( ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 ‖ F 2 ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\quantity[\norm{\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})-\quantity(\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}}_{F}^{2}]

 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ​ ( ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d − 𝐇 ~ ​ ( 𝜽 ) ) ​ ( ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 ‖ F 2 ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\quantity[\norm{\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})\quantity(\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d}-\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}}))\quantity(\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}}_{F}^{2}] 
 ≤ ( b ) \displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}

 where ( a ) (a) follows from the Resolvent identity 𝐀 − 1 − 𝐁 − 1 = 𝐀 − 1 ​ ( 𝐁 − 𝐀 ) ​ 𝐁 − 1 {\mathbf{A}}^{-1}-{\mathbf{B}}^{-1}={\mathbf{A}}^{-1}({\mathbf{B}}-{\mathbf{A}}){\mathbf{B}}^{-1} for any invertible matrices 𝐀 {\mathbf{A}} and 𝐁 {\mathbf{B}} , and ( b ) (b) follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. 
 We next bound these three terms in ( 79 ) respectively. For the first term, we have: 
 ‖ 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ 2 2 = ‖ 1 λ ​ 𝐈 d − ∑ k = 1 K ν k λ ​ ( λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ) ​ 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ ‖ 2 2 . \norm{\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})}_{2}^{2}=\norm{\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{I}}_{d}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\nu_{k}}{\lambda\quantity(\lambda(K-1)+\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2})}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}}_{2}^{2}\ . 
 (80) 
 Note that the summation ∑ k = 1 K ν k λ ​ ( λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ) ​ 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ \sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\nu_{k}}{\lambda\quantity(\lambda(K-1)+\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2})}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top} is a rank- K K matrix. Under the orthogonality assumption of random directions { u k } k = 1 K \{u_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K} , the eigenvalues of 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) \widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}}) related to the subspace spanned by { 𝐮 k } k = 1 K \{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K} can be computed as: 
 λ k = 1 λ − ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 λ ​ ( λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ) = K − 1 λ ​ ( K − 1 ) + ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 = 1 λ ​ ( 1 + t k ) , \lambda_{k}=\frac{1}{\lambda}-\frac{\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2}}{\lambda\quantity(\lambda(K-1)+\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2})}=\frac{K-1}{\lambda(K-1)+\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2}}=\frac{1}{\lambda\quantity(1+t_{k})}\ , 
 (81) 
 where we denote t k ≜ ν k ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 / ( λ ​ ( K − 1 ) ) t_{k}\triangleq\nu_{k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2}/\quantity(\lambda(K-1)) for simplicity. 
 Meanwhile, in the orthogonal complement subspace, the eigenvalues are all 1 / λ 1/\lambda . Therefore, we have: 
 ‖ 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ 2 2 = max ⁡ { 1 λ 2 , max k = 1 , … , K ⁡ 1 λ 2 ​ ( 1 + t k ) 2 } = 1 λ 2 ​ max ⁡ { 1 , max k = 1 , … , K ⁡ 1 ( 1 + t k ) 2 } . \norm{\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})}_{2}^{2}=\max\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}},\max_{k=1,\dots,K}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}(1+t_{k})^{2}}\right\}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\max\left\{1,\max_{k=1,\dots,K}\frac{1}{(1+t_{k})^{2}}\right\}\ . 
 (82) 
 To avoid singularity, we assume the existence of a positive constant ρ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \rho\in(0,1] such that | t k + 1 | ≥ ρ |t_{k}+1|\geq\rho for all k k . There are two cases to consider: 
 • 
 For case t k ≥ 0 t_{k}\geq 0 or t k ≤ − 2 t_{k}\leq-2 , we have 1 / ( 1 + t k ) 2 ≤ 1 1/(1+t_{k})^{2}\leq 1 , thus ‖ 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ 2 2 = 1 / λ 2 \norm{\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})}_{2}^{2}=1/\lambda^{2} . 
 •

 ‖ 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ 2 2 = max k = 1 , … , K ⁡ 1 λ 2 ​ ( 1 + t k ) 2 ≤ 1 λ 2 ​ ρ 2 . \norm{\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})}_{2}^{2}=\max_{k=1,\dots,K}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}(1+t_{k})^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}\rho^{2}}\ . 
 (83) 
 Overall, since ρ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \rho\in(0,1] , we have:

 (84)

 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d − 𝐇 ~ ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ F 2 ] ≤ K ​ d ​ ( d + 2 ) ​ V ( K − 1 ) 2 ​ μ 4 + L 2 2 ​ μ 2 ​ d \mathbb{E}\quantity[\norm{\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d}-\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})}_{F}^{2}]\leq\frac{Kd(d+2)V}{(K-1)^{2}\mu^{4}}+L_{2}^{2}\mu^{2}d 
 (85) 
 For the third term in ( 79 ): 
 ‖ ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 ‖ 2 2 ≤ ‖ ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 ‖ F 2 = ∑ i = 1 d 1 ( σ i + λ ) 2 ≤ d ( σ min + λ ) 2 , \displaystyle\norm{(\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}}_{2}^{2}\leq\norm{(\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}}_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\quantity(\sigma_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}\leq\frac{d}{\quantity(\sigma_{\min}+\lambda)^{2}}\ , 
 (86)

 C.14 Proof of Thm.  5.4

 Assume that the objective function is bounded, i.e. there exists a positive constant G G such that f ​ ( 𝛉 ; ξ ) ≤ G f({\bm{\theta}};\xi)\leq G for all 𝛉 ∈ ℝ d {\bm{\theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} and ξ \xi .  ‖ 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ​ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ 2 = ‖ ∑ k = 1 K μ ​ ν k λ ​ ( K − 1 ) ​ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 ​ = ( a ) ​ μ 2 λ 2 ​ ( K − 1 ) 2 ​ ∑ k = 1 K ν k 2 ​ ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 = K ​ μ 2 λ 2 ​ ( K − 1 ) 2 ​ ν 2 ​ ‖ 𝐮 ‖ 2 , \norm{\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}})}^{2}=\norm{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mu\nu_{k}}{\lambda(K-1)}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2}\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{\mu^{2}}{\lambda^{2}(K-1)^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu_{k}^{2}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2}=\frac{K\mu^{2}}{\lambda^{2}(K-1)^{2}}\nu^{2}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}}^{2}\ ,

 = \displaystyle=

 (96) 
 where we denote B ≜ 4 ​ K ​ G 2 ( K − 1 ) 2 ​ μ 6 ​ ( d + 2 ) ​ ( d + 4 ) B\triangleq\frac{4KG^{2}}{\quantity(K-1)^{2}\mu^{6}}\quantity(d+2)(d+4) , B 1 ≜ B μ 6 + L 2 2 ​ d B_{1}\triangleq\frac{B}{\mu^{6}}+L_{2}^{2}d , and B 2 ≜ B μ 6 ​ ( d + 3 ) + L 2 2 ​ d ​ ( d + 1 ) B_{2}\triangleq\frac{B}{\mu^{6}}\quantity(d+3)+L_{2}^{2}d(d+1) for simplicity.

 ‖ ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 ‖ 2 2 ≤ ‖ ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 ‖ F 2 = ∑ i = 1 d 1 ( σ i + λ ) 2 ≤ d ( σ min + λ ) 2 , \norm{(\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}}_{2}^{2}\leq\norm{(\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}}_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\quantity(\sigma_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}\leq\frac{d}{\quantity(\sigma_{\min}+\lambda)^{2}}\ , 
 (97) 
 where σ i \sigma_{i} denotes the i i -th eigenvalue of ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}}) , and σ min \sigma_{\min} denotes the smallest eigenvalue respectively. 
 Overall, we have: 
 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ​ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) − ( ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 ​ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ 2 ] ≤ K ​ d 2 ​ ( B 1 ​ σ ξ 2 + 4 ​ B 2 ​ L 0 2 ​ μ 2 ) λ 2 ​ ( σ min + λ ) 2 ​ ( K − 1 ) 2 . \mathbb{E}\quantity[\norm{\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}})-\quantity(\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}})}^{2}]\leq\frac{Kd^{2}\quantity(B_{1}\sigma_{\xi}^{2}+4B_{2}L_{0}^{2}\mu^{2})}{\lambda^{2}\quantity(\sigma_{\min}+\lambda)^{2}(K-1)^{2}}\ . 
 (98) 
 ∎

 Appendix D Additional Discussion 
 D.1 Equivalence between Prop.  3.2 with Averaged Baseline and Def.  4.1 
 The Gaussian smoothed Hessian estimator in Prop.  3.2 with the averaged baseline b = 𝔼 ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) ] b=\mathbb{E}\quantity[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)] is given as: 
 ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝔼 ξ ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ; ξ ) ] − 𝔼 ​ [ f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) ] μ 2 ​ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) ] . \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}};\xi)]-\mathbb{E}\quantity[f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)]}{\mu^{2}}\quantity({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d})\right]\ . 
 (108) 
 Moreover, the practical implementation (via Monte Carlo) of the above estimator is: 
 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 1 K − 1 ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) − 1 K ​ ∑ k ′ = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ; ξ ) μ 2 ​ ( 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) , \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})=\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)-\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}\quantity({\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d})\ , 
 (109) 
 where the 1 K − 1 \frac{1}{K-1} factor is used to make the estimator unbiased (similar to Def.  4.1 ). 
 The estimator ( 109 ) can be further decomposed as: 
 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) = \displaystyle\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})= 
 1 K − 1 ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) − 1 K ​ ∑ k ′ = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ; ξ ) μ 2 ​ 𝐮 k ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ ⏟ Def.  4.1 \displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)-\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}}_{\text{Def.~\ref{def:hess-est}}} 
 (110) 
 − 1 K − 1 ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) − 1 K ​ ∑ k ′ = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ; ξ ) μ 2 ​ 𝐈 d ⏟ = 0 , \displaystyle\quad-\underbrace{\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)-\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{I}}_{d}}_{=0}\ , 
 where the first term is exactly the Hessian estimator in Def.  4.1 , and the second term equals zero since: 
 1 K − 1 ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) − 1 K ​ ∑ k ′ = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ; ξ ) μ 2 ​ 𝐈 d \displaystyle\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)-\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{I}}_{d} 
 (111) 
 = \displaystyle= 
 ( 1 K − 1 ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) μ 2 − K K − 1 ​ 1 K ​ ∑ k ′ = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ′ ; ξ ) μ 2 ) ​ 𝐈 d \displaystyle\quantity(\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}-\frac{K}{K-1}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k^{\prime}};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}){\mathbf{I}}_{d} 
 = \displaystyle= 
 ( 1 K − 1 ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) μ 2 − 1 K − 1 ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 k ; ξ ) μ 2 ) ​ 𝐈 d = 0 . \displaystyle\quantity(\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}-\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}}_{k};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}){\mathbf{I}}_{d}=0\ . 
 Overall, we show that the Gaussian smoothed Hessian estimator in Prop.  3.2 with the averaged baseline is equivalent to the one in Def.  4.1 . 
 D.2 Smoothing Bias vs. Smoothing Radius μ \mu 
 The Hessian estimator in Thm.  4.6 is unbiased for the Gaussian-smoothed Hessian ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) rather than the exact Hessian ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}}) . The resulting smoothing bias between ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) and ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}}) is controlled directly by the smoothing radius μ \mu . Below we present the formal bound on the smoothing bias in terms of μ \mu : 
 Proposition D.1 (Bias of Smoothed Hessian) . 
 Under Assump.  4.3 , for 𝐮 ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 d ) {\mathbf{u}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) and F μ ​ ( 𝛉 ) = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ F ​ ( 𝛉 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ] F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}}[F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})] , the Gaussian-smoothed Hessian satisfies: 
 ‖ ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) − ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ F ≤ L 2 ​ μ ​ d . \norm{\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})-\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})}_{F}\leq L_{2}\mu\sqrt{d}\ . 
 (112) 
 Remark. 
Prop.  D.1 gives the unsquared version of Lem.  C.3 , thus the proof is similar. The bound in Prop.  D.1 explicitly characterizes the dependence of the smoothing bias on the smoothing radius μ \mu . Thus, decreasing μ \mu reduces the gap between the smoothed and true Hessians linearly. At the same time, the finite-sample variance terms (Thm.  4.8 ) in these bounds contain inverse powers of μ \mu , so μ \mu should be interpreted as a bias-variance tuning parameter rather than simply set as small as possible in noisy finite-sample estimation. 
 Empirical Validation. 
 We also empirically evaluate the Frobenius norm gap ‖ ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) − ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ F \norm{\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})-\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})}_{F} under different smoothing radii on Rosenbrock and Styblinski-Tang functions to validate Prop.  D.1 . 
 As shown in Fig.  4 , the bias (Frobenius norm gap) proportionally increases with μ \mu in a log-log scale, with slopes approximately around 1 1 , matching the linear dependence predicted by Prop.  D.1 . These results indicate that the smoothing bias is well controlled for sufficiently small μ \mu . 
 Figure 4 : Frobenius norm error ‖ ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) − ∇ 2 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ F \norm{\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})-\nabla^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})}_{F} under different smoothing radii μ \mu . The log-log slopes are 1.04 1.04 for Rosenbrock and 1.01 1.01 for Styblinski-Tang. The errors are averaged over 3 3 independent runs. Since the standard deviations are small, we omit the error bars for better visualization. 
 D.3 Connection Between Optimal Baseline and Control Variates 
 The optimal baseline result in Thm.  4.7 can be interpreted as a direct analogue of the classical control variate principle  (Asmussen and Glynn, 2007 ) .
For the Gaussian Hessian identity in Prop.  3.2 , define the random matrix variables: 
 𝐀 ​ ( 𝐮 ) ≜ F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) μ 2 ​ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) , 𝐁 ​ ( 𝐮 ) ≜ 1 μ 2 ​ ( 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ) . {\mathbf{A}}({\mathbf{u}})\triangleq\frac{F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})}{\mu^{2}}\quantity({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}),\qquad{\mathbf{B}}({\mathbf{u}})\triangleq\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\quantity({\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d})\ . 
 (113) 
 Since 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝐁 ​ ( 𝐮 ) ] = 𝟎 \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}[{\mathbf{B}}({\mathbf{u}})]={\bm{0}} , the baseline estimator can be written as 𝐀 ​ ( 𝐮 ) − b ​ 𝐁 ​ ( 𝐮 ) {\mathbf{A}}({\mathbf{u}})-b{\mathbf{B}}({\mathbf{u}}) and remains unbiased for any constant b b that is independent of 𝐮 {\mathbf{u}} : 
 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝐀 ​ ( 𝐮 ) − b ​ 𝐁 ​ ( 𝐮 ) ] = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝐀 ​ ( 𝐮 ) ] = ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) . \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}[{\mathbf{A}}({\mathbf{u}})-b{\mathbf{B}}({\mathbf{u}})]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}[{\mathbf{A}}({\mathbf{u}})]=\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})\ . 
 (114) 
 Thus, the baseline term is a zero-mean control variate whose coefficient is chosen only to reduce variance, not to change the target expectation. 
 Because the estimator is matrix-valued, the natural scalar criterion is the Frobenius variance, 
 min b ∈ ℝ ⁡ 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ‖ 𝐀 ​ ( 𝐮 ) − b ​ 𝐁 ​ ( 𝐮 ) − ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ F 2 ] . \min_{b\in{\mathbb{R}}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\quantity[\norm{{\mathbf{A}}({\mathbf{u}})-b{\mathbf{B}}({\mathbf{u}})-\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}})}_{F}^{2}]\ . 
 (115) 
 Differentiating with respect to b b gives 
 b ∗ = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ⟨ 𝐀 ​ ( 𝐮 ) − ∇ 2 F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) , 𝐁 ​ ( 𝐮 ) ⟩ F ] 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ‖ 𝐁 ​ ( 𝐮 ) ‖ F 2 ] = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ⟨ 𝐀 ​ ( 𝐮 ) , 𝐁 ​ ( 𝐮 ) ⟩ F ] 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ‖ 𝐁 ​ ( 𝐮 ) ‖ F 2 ] = 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ F ​ ( 𝜽 + μ ​ 𝐮 ) ​ ‖ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ‖ F 2 ] 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ ‖ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ‖ F 2 ] , b^{*}=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\quantity[\left\langle{\mathbf{A}}({\mathbf{u}})-\nabla^{2}F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}),{\mathbf{B}}({\mathbf{u}})\right\rangle_{F}]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\quantity[\norm{{\mathbf{B}}({\mathbf{u}})}_{F}^{2}]}=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\quantity[\left\langle{\mathbf{A}}({\mathbf{u}}),{\mathbf{B}}({\mathbf{u}})\right\rangle_{F}]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}\quantity[\norm{{\mathbf{B}}({\mathbf{u}})}_{F}^{2}]}=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}}\quantity[F({\bm{\theta}}+\mu{\mathbf{u}})\norm{{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}}_{F}^{2}]}{\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{u}}}\quantity[\norm{{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}}_{F}^{2}]}\ , 
 (116) 
 where the second equality uses 𝔼 𝐮 ​ [ 𝐁 ​ ( 𝐮 ) ] = 𝟎 \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{u}}[{\mathbf{B}}({\mathbf{u}})]={\bm{0}} .
This is exactly the optimal baseline in ( 13 ).
When ‖ 𝐮𝐮 ⊤ − 𝐈 d ‖ F 2 \norm{{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{I}}_{d}}_{F}^{2} concentrates in high dimensions, the coefficient reduces to F μ ​ ( 𝜽 ) F_{\mu}({\bm{\theta}}) , which is estimated in practice by the averaged baseline. 
 D.4 Exact Woodbury Inverse vs. Approximate Inverse Hessian 
 The inverse Hessian approximation in Def.  5.1 replaces the full Gram matrix 𝐔 ⊤ ​ 𝐔 {\mathbf{U}}^{\top}{\mathbf{U}} in the exact Woodbury inverse with its diagonal part. This step is exact when the random directions are pairwise orthogonal. For Gaussian directions, it is also accurate in high dimensions because the diagonal terms ‖ 𝐮 k ‖ 2 \norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}^{2} are of order d d , while the off-diagonal terms 𝐮 i ⊤ ​ 𝐮 j {\mathbf{u}}_{i}^{\top}{\mathbf{u}}_{j} are only of order d \sqrt{d} for i ≠ j i\neq j . The following proposition gives the resulting approximation error. 
 Proposition D.2 (Exact and Approximate Inverse Error) . 
 Let 𝐔 = [ 𝐮 1 , … , 𝐮 K ] ∈ ℝ d × K {\mathbf{U}}=[{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}_{K}]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times K} with 𝐮 k ∼ i . i . d . 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 d ) {\mathbf{u}}_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\mathbf{I}}_{d}) . Define 𝐆 ≜ 𝐔 ⊤ ​ 𝐔 {\mathbf{G}}\triangleq{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}{\mathbf{U}} , Δ ≜ diag ⁡ ( 𝐆 ) \Delta\triangleq\operatorname{diag}({\mathbf{G}}) , and 𝐄 ≜ 𝐆 − Δ {\mathbf{E}}\triangleq{\mathbf{G}}-\Delta . Let 
 𝐌 ≜ 𝐃 − 1 + 1 λ ​ 𝐆 , 𝐌 ~ ≜ 𝐃 − 1 + 1 λ ​ Δ , {\mathbf{M}}\triangleq{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}+\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{G}},\quad\widetilde{{\mathbf{M}}}\triangleq{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}+\frac{1}{\lambda}\Delta\ , 
 (117) 
 where 𝐃 {\mathbf{D}} is defined as in Def.  5.1 . Assume λ > 0 \lambda>0 is fixed, ‖ 𝐃 − 1 ‖ 2 = 𝒪 p ​ ( 1 ) \norm{{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}}_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(1) . Then, 
 ‖ ( 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 − 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ F = 𝒪 p ​ ( K d ) . \norm{\quantity(\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}-\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})}_{F}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\quantity(\frac{K}{\sqrt{d}})\ . 
 (118) 
 Proof. 
 From ( 14 ) and ( 15 ), the only difference between the exact inverse and the approximation is whether 𝐌 − 1 {\mathbf{M}}^{-1} or 𝐌 ~ − 1 \widetilde{{\mathbf{M}}}^{-1} is used in the low-dimensional Woodbury correction: 
 ( 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 − 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) = − 1 λ 2 ​ 𝐔 ​ ( 𝐌 − 1 − 𝐌 ~ − 1 ) ​ 𝐔 ⊤ . \quantity(\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}-\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})=-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}{\mathbf{U}}\quantity({\mathbf{M}}^{-1}-\widetilde{{\mathbf{M}}}^{-1}){\mathbf{U}}^{\top}\ . 
 (119) 
 For Gaussian directions, standard concentration gives ‖ 𝐔 ‖ 2 2 = ‖ 𝐆 ‖ 2 = 𝒪 p ​ ( d ) \norm{{\mathbf{U}}}_{2}^{2}=\norm{{\mathbf{G}}}_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(d) , ‖ Δ ‖ 2 = Θ p ​ ( d ) \norm{\Delta}_{2}=\Theta_{p}(d) , and ‖ 𝐄 ‖ F = 𝒪 p ​ ( K ​ d ) \norm{{\mathbf{E}}}_{F}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(K\sqrt{d}) , since the off-diagonal entries 𝐮 i ⊤ ​ 𝐮 j {\mathbf{u}}_{i}^{\top}{\mathbf{u}}_{j} have variance d d for i ≠ j i\neq j . Because ‖ 𝐃 − 1 ‖ 2 = 𝒪 p ​ ( 1 ) \norm{{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}}_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(1) and λ \lambda is fixed, the diagonal matrix 𝐌 ~ \widetilde{{\mathbf{M}}} has entries of order d d , hence ‖ 𝐌 ~ − 1 ‖ 2 = 𝒪 p ​ ( d − 1 ) \norm{\widetilde{{\mathbf{M}}}^{-1}}_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(d^{-1}) . 
 By the assumed stability of the exact Woodbury inverse, ‖ 𝐌 − 1 ‖ 2 = 𝒪 p ​ ( d − 1 ) \norm{{\mathbf{M}}^{-1}}_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(d^{-1}) . Then, by the resolvent identity, 
 𝐌 − 1 − 𝐌 ~ − 1 = − 1 λ ​ 𝐌 − 1 ​ 𝐄 ​ 𝐌 ~ − 1 . {\mathbf{M}}^{-1}-\widetilde{{\mathbf{M}}}^{-1}=-\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbf{M}}^{-1}{\mathbf{E}}\widetilde{{\mathbf{M}}}^{-1}. 
 (120) 
 Therefore, 
 ‖ ( 𝐇 ^ ​ ( 𝜽 ) + λ ​ 𝐈 d ) − 1 − 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ‖ F ≤ 1 λ 3 ​ ‖ 𝐔 ‖ 2 2 ​ ‖ 𝐌 − 1 ‖ 2 ​ ‖ 𝐄 ‖ F ​ ‖ 𝐌 ~ − 1 ‖ 2 \displaystyle\norm{\quantity(\widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}({\bm{\theta}})+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{d})^{-1}-\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})}_{F}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\norm{{\mathbf{U}}}_{2}^{2}\norm{{\mathbf{M}}^{-1}}_{2}\norm{{\mathbf{E}}}_{F}\norm{\widetilde{{\mathbf{M}}}^{-1}}_{2} 
 (121) 
 = \displaystyle= 
 𝒪 p ​ ( d ) ⋅ 𝒪 p ​ ( d − 1 ) ⋅ 𝒪 p ​ ( K ​ d ) ⋅ 𝒪 p ​ ( d − 1 ) = 𝒪 p ​ ( K d ) , \displaystyle\ \mathcal{O}_{p}(d)\cdot\mathcal{O}_{p}(d^{-1})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{p}(K\sqrt{d})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{p}(d^{-1})=\mathcal{O}_{p}\quantity(\frac{K}{\sqrt{d}})\ , 
 which completes the proof.
∎ 
 Remark. 
Prop.  D.2 formalizes the intuition behind the diagonal Gram approximation in ( 15 ). The result keeps the dependence on the number of query directions as 𝒪 p ​ ( K / d ) \mathcal{O}_{p}(K/\sqrt{d}) . For the small fixed K K used in our experiments, this reduces to the d − 1 / 2 d^{-1/2} decay observed empirically. Thus, the approximation becomes increasingly accurate in the high-dimensional regimes targeted by ZoVH, while avoiding the need to manipulate dense d × d d\times d inverse matrices. 
 Empirical Validation. 
 We further empirically measure the Frobenius norm error between the exact Woodbury inverse in ( 14 ) and the approximation in ( 15 ). 
 Figure 5 : Frobenius norm error between the exact Woodbury inverse and the approximate inverse as the dimension d d increases. We use K = 3 K=3 and average the result over 50 50 random seeds. 
 As shown in Fig.  5 , the error decreases consistently as the dimension increases. The fitted slopes are close to − 0.5 -0.5 across four synthetic functions, matching the d − 1 / 2 d^{-1/2} dependence in Prop.  D.2 . The result supports using the approximate inverse for the small K K setting used by ZoVH, especially in LLM fine-tuning where individual layers contain millions of parameters. 
 D.5 Inverse Hessian-Gradient Product Approximation with Query Reuse 
 Definition D.3 (Shared Product with Query Reuse) . 
 Assume 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) \widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}}) and ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}}) are estimated using the same set of standard Gaussian directions { 𝐮 k } k = 1 K \{{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K} . The bias-corrected inverse Hessian-gradient product 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ​ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 ) \widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}})\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}}) with query reuse is given by: 
 𝐇 ~ − 1 ​ ( 𝜽 t − 1 ) ​ ∇ ^ ​ F ​ ( 𝜽 t − 1 ) ≜ ∑ i , k = 1 N , K μ ​ ν t − i , k λ ​ ( 1 N ​ K − 1 − 𝐮 t − i , k ⊤ ​ ( ∑ ( j , k ′ ) ≠ ( i , k ) ν t − j , k ′ ​ 𝐮 t − j , k ′ ) / ( N ​ K − 2 ) λ ​ ( N ​ K − 1 ) + ν t − i , k ​ ‖ 𝐮 t − i , k ‖ 2 ) ​ 𝐮 t − i , k . \widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{-1}({\bm{\theta}}_{t-1})\hat{\nabla}F({\bm{\theta}}_{t-1})\triangleq\sum_{i,k=1}^{N,K}\frac{\mu\nu_{t-i,k}}{\lambda}\quantity(\frac{1}{NK-1}-\frac{{\mathbf{u}}_{t-i,k}^{\top}\left(\sum\limits_{(j,k^{\prime})\neq(i,k)}\nu_{t-j,k^{\prime}}{\mathbf{u}}_{t-j,k^{\prime}}\right)/(NK-2)}{\lambda(NK-1)+\nu_{t-i,k}\norm{{\mathbf{u}}_{t-i,k}}^{2}}){\mathbf{u}}_{t-i,k}. 
 (122) 
 where the averaged baseline b ^ t ≜ 1 N ​ K ​ ∑ i , k = 1 N , K y t − i , k \hat{b}_{t}\triangleq\frac{1}{NK}\sum_{i,k=1}^{N,K}y_{t-i,k} , and ν t − i , k ≜ ( y t − i , k − b ^ t ) / μ 2 \nu_{t-i,k}\triangleq\quantity(y_{t-i,k}-\hat{b}_{t})/\mu^{2} . 
 Remark. The definition above extends Def.  5.3 to incorporate query reuse across N N iterations. By reusing the same set of K K random directions over N N consecutive iterations, we effectively increase the sample size to N ​ K NK , which enhances the accuracy of both the Hessian and gradient estimations. This approach leverages historical query information, reducing the need for additional function evaluations while maintaining robust optimization performance. 
 D.6 Comparison with HiZOO  (Zhao et al. , 2025 ) 
 HiZOO  (Zhao et al. , 2025 ) is a recently proposed curvature-aware zeroth-order optimization method that utilizes a randomized central-difference Hessian estimator ( 5 ) for memory-efficient LLMs fine-tuning. However, to avoid the computational complexity of inverting the full Hessian estimator, HiZOO only approximates the diagonal of the Hessian estimator to facilitate inversion. Besides, recent studies  (Zhang et al. , 2024 ) have shown that the practical Hessian of LLMs exhibits significant block-diagonal structures, indicating that simple diagonal approximations may overlook important curvature information. 
 In contrast, our proposed method ZoVH addresses this by approximating the full Hessian matrix and captures more comprehensive curvature information than diagonal approximations. This leads to more accurate inverse Hessian-gradient products, which are crucial for effective optimization steps. A detailed comparison of HiZOO and ZoVH in terms of Hessian approximation and its inverse is summarized in Table  2 .

 Eq. ( 6 )

 𝐇 ^ diag ​ ( 𝜽 ) ≜ 1 2 ​ K ​ ∑ k = 1 K f ​ ( 𝜽 k + ; ξ ) − 2 ​ f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) + f ​ ( 𝜽 k − ; ξ ) μ 2 ​ 𝐮 k ⊤ ​ 𝐮 k \widehat{{\mathbf{H}}}_{\text{diag}}({\bm{\theta}})\triangleq\frac{1}{2K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{f({\bm{\theta}}^{+}_{k};\xi)-2f({\bm{\theta}};\xi)+f({\bm{\theta}}^{-}_{k};\xi)}{\mu^{2}}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}^{\top}{\mathbf{u}}_{k}

 Appendix E Additional Experiments 
 E.1 Curvature-Aware Zeroth-Order LLM Fine-Tuning 
 Recent advancements in memory-efficient fine-tuning for large language models (LLMs) have begun to leverage zeroth-order optimization  (Malladi et al. , 2023 ) and curvature-aware techniques  (Zhao et al. , 2025 ) .
However, conventional ZO Hessian approximations often suffer from high variance, which can hamper convergence during fine-tuning.
ZoVH addresses this limitation by reducing variance through a provably averaged baseline and the reuse of historical query information.
In this section, we apply ZoVH to curvature-aware ZO fine-tuning of LLMs, comparing its performance against MeZO  (Malladi et al. , 2023 ) and HiZOO  (Zhao et al. , 2025 ) (detailed in Appx.  F.4 ).
Specifically, we fine-tune OPT-1.3B and OPT-13B  (Zhang et al. , 2022 ) on five downstream tasks from the GLUE  (Wang et al. , 2018 ) and SuperGLUE  (Wang et al. , 2020 ) benchmarks, including SST2, COPA, SQuAD, WSC, and WIC. 
 Figure 6 : Convergence comparison of different methods for curvature-aware ZO fine-tuning on OPT-1.3B and OPT-13B. 
 Results. 
 As shown in Fig.  6 , ZoVH exhibits faster convergence than both MeZO and HiZOO across distinct tasks and model sizes.
Furthermore, ZoVH with N = 2 N=2 (incorporating query reuse) accelerates convergence further while achieving test accuracy comparable to, or even exceeding, that of ZoVH with N = 1 N=1 (without query reuse).
The test accuracy for all methods is summarized in Tab.  3 .
ZoVH secures the highest accuracy on the majority of tasks, as well as the highest average accuracy across both models.
Notably, ZoVH with N = 2 N=2 results in lower accuracy on certain tasks compared to N = 1 N=1 . This reflects the bias-variance tradeoff inherent in query reuse, where bias may eventually dominate the total error near convergence.
In practice, this issue can be alleviated by reducing the reuse parameter N N as the optimization approaches convergence.

 74.55 
 It is worth noting that HiZOO demonstrates inferior performance compared to MeZO in both convergence speed and test accuracy.
This underperformance is likely due to the Hessian smoothing factor α \alpha in HiZOO, which is set to a small value (e.g., α = 10 − 8 \alpha=10^{-8} , refer to Appx.  F.4 ) to guarantee numerical stability when inverting the diagonal Hessian estimator (while higher α \alpha values leads to divergence issues in our experiments).
Consequently, such a small α \alpha may provide negligible regularization, leading to behavior that is practically indistinguishable from MeZO.
Additionally, HiZOO requires 3 3 function queries per step, whereas MeZO uses only 2 2 .
Since we evaluate performance under a fixed query budget (as shown in Fig.  6 and Tab.  3 ), HiZOO completes fewer optimization steps, further contributing to its degraded performance. 
 Runtime and Memory Overhead. 
 We also measure wall-clock running time and peak GPU memory on the OPT-13B fine-tuning experiments to evaluate the practical overhead of the inverse Hessian-gradient product in Def.  D.3 .
Tab.  4 reports the results. 
 As shown in Tab.  4 , ZoVH is close to HiZOO in both running time and peak memory usage, while being only slightly slower than MeZO.
Compared with HiZOO, ZoVH increases running time by about 5 % 5\% and peak memory by only 0.04 0.04 GB.
The wall-clock overhead remains small because forward evaluations dominate the runtime, and the additional inverse-Hessian computation only uses scalar coefficients and random directions reconstructed from their seeds.
The memory overhead is also limited, since the history buffer stores only N ​ K NK scalar function values and their corresponding random seeds, rather than the full perturbation vectors.
Its persistent memory cost is therefore 𝒪 ​ ( N ​ K ) \mathcal{O}(NK) and does not grow with the model dimension.

 ZoVH

 27.16 
 E.2 Ablation Study for Averaged Baseline and Query Reuse 
 We further isolate the individual contributions of the averaged baseline and query reuse in ZoVH.
The ablation is conducted on four synthetic optimization benchmarks with d = 5000 d=5000 .
We compare four variants that differ only in the baseline choice and whether query reuse is enabled.
The anchor baseline variants use b = F ​ ( 𝜽 ) b=F({\bm{\theta}}) , while the averaged baseline variants use ( 8 ).
The query reuse variants use the same history setting as the synthetic optimization experiments with N = 4 N=4 . 
 Figure 7 : Ablation of the averaged baseline and query reuse on synthetic optimization benchmarks with d = 5000 d=5000 and K = 3 K=3 . Results are averaged over 5 5 independent runs. Lower optimality gap is better. 
 As shown in Fig.  7 , most of the gain comes from replacing the anchor baseline with the averaged baseline, regardless of whether query reuse is used.
The averaged baseline curves descend much faster and reach substantially lower optimality gaps than their anchor baseline counterparts.
This supports the variance-minimization role of the averaged baseline established in Thm.  4.7 .
Query reuse provides an additional gain by enlarging the effective sample size without adding new function evaluations, especially in the early and middle stages of optimization.
The best performance is achieved when the averaged baseline and query reuse are combined.
This is consistent with the bias-variance discussion in Thm.  B.1 , where a moderate history length reduces variance while the iterate drift remains small enough that the additional reuse bias does not dominate. 
 Appendix F Experiments Setup 
 F.1 Empirical Error Analysis of Hessian Approximation 
 Baselines. 
 The compared estimators include the two-point ( 3 ), three-point ( 4 ) Stein Estimators, the randomized central-difference estimator ( 5 ), and our proposed ZoVH w/ and w/o the query reuse technique.
The one-point Stein estimator ( 2 ) is excluded from the comparison due to its significantly higher error than other methods. 
 Synthetic Functions. 
 All experiments are conducted in d = 5000 d=5000 dimensions, and averaged over 500 500 test points Hessian errors ( 20 20 random initializations with 25 25 test points collected along the optimization trajectory). The analytical forms of the synthetic functions used in our experiments are as follows: 
 • 
 Quadratic Function: 
 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 1 2 ​ 𝜽 T ​ 𝐀 ​ 𝜽 + 𝒃 T ​ 𝜽 + c , F({\bm{\theta}})=\frac{1}{2}{\bm{\theta}}^{T}{\mathbf{A}}{\bm{\theta}}+{\bm{b}}^{T}{\bm{\theta}}+c\ , 
 (123) 
 where 𝐀 {\mathbf{A}} is a symmetric positive-definite matrix, b b is a d d -dimensional vector, and c c is a scalar constant. 
 • 
 Rosenbrock Function: 
 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) = ∑ i = 1 d − 1 [ 100 ​ ( θ i + 1 − θ i 2 ) 2 + ( 1 − θ i ) 2 ] . F({\bm{\theta}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left[100(\theta_{i+1}-\theta_{i}^{2})^{2}+(1-\theta_{i})^{2}\right]\ . 
 (124) 
 • 
 Styblinski-Tang Function: 
 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ i = 1 d ( θ i 4 − 16 ​ θ i 2 + 50 ​ θ i ) . F({\bm{\theta}})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(\theta_{i}^{4}-16\theta_{i}^{2}+50\theta_{i}\right)\ . 
 (125) 
 Neural Network Hessian. 
 We further evaluate the Hessian approximation error on a small convolutional neural network (CNN), which consists of two convolutional layers, followed by two fully connected layers.
In Fig.  3 , we present the empirical Hessian approximation errors across the first convolutional layer ( d = 96 d=96 ), the second convolutional layer ( d = 6144 d=6144 ), and the second fully connected layer ( d = 1280 d=1280 ).
This model is trained on the MNIST dataset  (LeCun et al. , 1998 ) for digit classification.
All experiments are averaged over 5625 5625 test points Hessian errors ( 3 3 independent runs with 1875 1875 test points collected along each optimization trajectory). 
 F.2 Synthetic Function Optimization 
 Baselines. 
 We compare ZoVH with several representative ZO optimization methods as baselines: 
 • 
 Vanilla ZOO  (Nesterov and Spokoiny, 2017 ) . This is the standard stochastic finite-difference gradient estimation, whose analytical form is similar to ( 6 ), except the averaged baseline b b replaced with anchor baseline b = f ​ ( 𝜽 ; ξ ) b=f({\bm{\theta}};\xi) . 
 • 
 HiZOO  (Zhao et al. , 2025 ) . This is a recent curvature-aware ZO optimization method that utilizes a randomized central-difference Hessian estimator ( 5 ) with diagonal approximation for LLMs fine-tuning. The complete comparison between HiZOO and ZoVH is detailed in Appx.  D.6 .
We do not include ZOHA  (Ye et al. , 2025 ) as a separate baseline because HiZOO already covers this case. When the scaling factor is set to 1 1 , HiZOO reduces to ZOHA. 
 • 
 ZoAR  (Qiu et al. , 2025 ) . This is a variance-reduced ZO optimization method that incorporates averaged baseline and query reuse techniques to improve gradient estimation. 
 Hyperparameter Settings. 
 All experiments are conducted in d = 10000 d=10000 dimensions, and averaged over independent 10 10 runs. To ensure fair comparison, the hyperparameters of
all methods are chosen to achieve the best performance (summarized in Tab.  5 ). The analytical forms of the synthetic functions used in our experiments are as follows: 
 • 
 Quadratic Function: 
 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ i = 1 d θ i 2 . F({\bm{\theta}})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\theta_{i}^{2}\ . 
 (126) 
 • 
 Levy Function: 
 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) = sin 2 ⁡ ( π ​ w 1 ) + ( w d − 1 ) 2 ​ ( 1 + sin 2 ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ w d ) ) + ∑ i = 1 d − 1 ( w i − 1 ) 2 ​ ( 1 + 10 ​ sin 2 ⁡ ( π ​ w i + 1 ) ) , F({\bm{\theta}})=\sin^{2}(\pi w_{1})+(w_{d}-1)^{2}(1+\sin^{2}(2\pi w_{d}))+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}(w_{i}-1)^{2}(1+10\sin^{2}(\pi w_{i}+1))\ , 
 (127) 
 where w i = 1 + θ i − 1 4 w_{i}=1+\frac{\theta_{i}-1}{4} . 
 • 
 Rosenbrock Function: 
 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) = ∑ i = 1 d − 1 [ 100 ​ ( θ i + 1 − θ i 2 ) 2 + ( 1 − θ i ) 2 ] . F({\bm{\theta}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left[100(\theta_{i+1}-\theta_{i}^{2})^{2}+(1-\theta_{i})^{2}\right]\ . 
 (128) 
 • 
 Ackley Function: 
 F ​ ( 𝜽 ) = − 20 ​ exp ⁡ ( − 0.2 ​ 1 d ​ ∑ i = 1 d θ i 2 ) − exp ⁡ ( 1 d ​ ∑ i = 1 d cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ θ i ) ) + 20 + e . F({\bm{\theta}})=-20\exp\left(-0.2\sqrt{\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\theta_{i}^{2}}\right)-\exp\left(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\cos(2\pi\theta_{i})\right)+20+e\ . 
 (129)

 F.3 Black-Box Adversarial Attack 
 Baselines. 
 The compared methods are the same as in the synthetic experiments in Sec.  F.2 . 
 Hyperparameter Settings. 
 For the black-box adversarial attack, we use the same model as in  (Shu et al. , 2025 ) : a simple two-layer CNN trained on the MNIST dataset. All experiments are averaged over 10 10 independent runs. To ensure fair comparison, all hyperparameters are chosen to achieve the best performance of each method (summarized in Tab.  6 ).

 F.4 Curvature-Aware Zeroth-Order LLM Fine-tuning 
 Baselines. 
 We compare ZoVH with two representative ZO optimization methods as baselines: 
 • 
 MeZO  (Malladi et al. , 2023 ) . A memory-efficient ZO fine-tuning method for LLMs that employs a central-difference gradient estimator. 
 • 
 HiZOO  (Zhao et al. , 2025 ) . A recent curvature-aware ZO optimization method that leverages a randomized central-difference Hessian estimator ( 5 ) with a diagonal approximation for LLM fine-tuning. A comprehensive comparison between HiZOO and ZoVH is provided in Appx.  D.6 .
We do not include ZOHA  (Ye et al. , 2025 ) as a separate baseline because HiZOO already covers this case. When the scaling factor is set to 1 1 , HiZOO reduces to ZOHA. 
 Hyperparameter Settings. 
 All experiments are averaged over 3 3 independent runs. To ensure fair comparison, all hyperparameters are chosen to achieve the best performance of each method (summarized in Tab.  7 ).
